A Quantum Field Theory for the interaction of
pions and rhos

Center for Theoretical & Mathematical Physics

A Quantum Field Theory for the interaction of pions and rhos

Preshin Moodley

Department of Physics

University of Cape Town

Abstract

We extend the Kroll-Lee-Zumino model in its particle content to include the charged rho vector mesons and the
neutral pion meson. This entailed using the larger SU(2) gauge group. The masses for the vector mesons were generated
via spontaneous symmetry breaking using the Higgs mechanism. The Lagrangian was then quantized and gauge fixed using the generalized class of
R_{\xi}
gauges. Tree scattering lengths were calculated for pion-pion scattering and the values for the
a^{0}_{0}
and
a^{2}_{0}
scattering lengths are found to be comparable with experiment. The one particle irreducible diagrams that contribute to the one loop corrections to the tree scattering lengths are renormalized.

1
Introduction

𝕊
oon after the success of Quantum Electrodynamics (QED) as a description of the electromagnetic interaction, physicists turned to the investigation of the structure of nucleons and the strong force. Yukawa’s conjecture
[
1
]
about the existence of a massive particle responsible for mediating the strong interaction was partially successful at explaining the low energy interactions of the nucleons. Numerous new particles were discovered during this period with seemingly no order among them. The quark model was developed by Gell-Man
[
2
]
and Zweig
[
3
,
4
]
to tame and show some regularity in the particle zoo with hadronic multiplets. Further investigations lead to the developement of the Parton model by Feynman
[
5
,
6
]
which assumed the nucleons were made up of essentially free particles called partons. A nagging question at the time was where were these partons or quarks? The phenomenon of Bjorken scaling
[
7
]
incited a search for quantum field theories which exhibited this behaviour. This ended with the discovery of asymptotic freedom in non-Abelian gauge theories by Politzer
[
8
]
, Wilczek and Gross
[
9
]
and leading to the formulation of Quantum Chromodynamics (QCD) which is a SU(3)
c
non-Abelian gauge with the new isospin degree of freedom identified as color charge. The partons were identified as the quarks and gluons of QCD and were forever bound in colorless unions.

The property of asymptotic freedom was fortunate since it allowed for the analysis of systems of high energy where the strong coupling has a relatively small value and conventional tools like perturbation theory were applicable. New tools needed to be forged to explore low energy systems. The direct approach taken in Lattice QCD has become feasible in recent years with the reduction in the costs of the massive computing power required and the steady increase in computing power. The alternate approach came with the development of models and effective field theories applicable in certain energy regimes. Chiral Perturbation Theory (
\chi
PT) is
the effective field theory for QCD and has been used for applications up to
\sim 300
MeV with a failure beyond
\sim 600
MeV

[
10
]
. The onset of Pertubative QCD has been pushed down to
1
GeV for some applications
[
11
]
. There exists
this gap of ignorance in the interval
\sim\left(0.6,1\right)
GeV for which standard analytic tools can not explore. This energy
region is dominated by the low energy scalar and vector mesons.

Nambu had suggested that the rho meson could explain the nucleon form factors
[
12
]
. Sakurai proposed the idea of Vector meson dominance (VMD) where the strong interaction would be
mediated by vector mesons in a non-Abelian gauge theory
[
13
,
14
]
. The idea of VMD is stated generally as: gauge bosons transforms into the lowest energy vector mesons and interact with hadrons through effective vertices.

Figure 1.1:
Photon transforming into a Rho which decays into pions.

The photon seems to interact with hadronic matter mainly through the rho vector meson
[
15
,
16
]
. Sakurai had suggested interaction terms for VMD but this was not a field theory which could be used for higher order loop analysis. Kroll, Lee and Zumino suggested a model
[
17
]
involving the charged pions and neutral rho as a candidate for VMD.

1.1
The Kroll-Lee-Zumino Model

The Kroll-Lee-Zumino Model (KLZ) is a quantum field theory developed to describe the interactions of charged pions and the neutral rho.

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}={\left({{\partial_{\mu}}\phi}\right)^{*}}{\partial^{\mu}}\phi-{m^{2}}{\phi^{*}}\phi-\frac{1}{4}{F_{\mu\nu}}{F^{\mu\nu}}+\frac{1}{2}{M^{2}}{A_{\mu}}{A^{\mu}}+ie{A^{\mu}}\left({{\phi^{*}}{\partial_{\mu}}\phi-\phi{\partial_{\mu}}{\phi^{*}}}\right)+{e^{2}}{\phi^{*}}\phi{A_{\mu}}{A^{\mu}}

(1.1.1)

with the field strength tensor
F_{\mu\nu}
defined as

\displaystyle{F_{\mu\nu}}:={\partial_{\mu}}{A_{\nu}}-{\partial_{\nu}}{A_{\mu}}

(1.1.2)

Here the pions are represented by
\phi
, with
m
the pion mass. The neutral rho is represented by
A_{\mu}
, with
M
the rho mass and
e
represents the strength of the rho-pion coupling which has an approximate value of
e\approx 5.96
. The KLZ is a renormalizable quantum field theory despite the breaking of U(1) gauge symmetry with the presence of the explicit mass term for the vector rho. This is a result of the vector field coupling only to the conserved current
[
18
,
19
]
i.e.

\displaystyle{J_{\mu}}

\displaystyle:={\phi^{*}}{\partial_{\mu}}\phi-\phi{\partial_{\mu}}{\phi^{*}}

(1.1.3)

with

\displaystyle{\partial^{\mu}}{J_{\mu}}

\displaystyle=0

(1.1.4)

This can be seen by looking at the propagator of a massive vector field

\displaystyle i{D_{\mu\nu}}=\frac{{-i}}{{{k^{2}}-{M^{2}}+i\varepsilon}}\left({{g_{\mu\nu}}-\frac{{{k_{\mu}}{k_{\nu}}}}{{{M^{2}}}}}\right)

(1.1.5)

which due to the
{k_{\mu}}{k_{\nu}}
term is usually logarithmically divergent. For the case of the KLZ, the above propagator would appear between conserved currents with

\displaystyle{k^{\mu}}{J_{\mu}}

\displaystyle=0

(1.1.6)

using (
1.1.4
) in the momentum representation, thus allowing for renormalizability by removing the logarithmically divergent terms. The KLZ being renormalizable makes it an attractive model for analysis, since the renomalizability allows for a systematic calculation of higher order loop corrections without introducing additional parameters into the model. Since the pertubative series expansion parameter is of the form
\dfrac{e}{4\pi}\approx 0.47
, higher order analysis can be pursued seriously. This can be seen in the application of the KLZ in finite temperature calculations undertaken in
[
20
]
. The KLZ has also been used to analyse the pion form factor by
[
21
]
, for space-like transferred momentum, the one loop correction to the form factor is in agreement with data with
\chi^{2}_{F}\approx 1.1
as shown in figure
1.2
.

Figure 1.2:
Pion form factor for
q^{2}<0

For time-like transferred momentum the KLZ follows the data closely below the rho peak but starts to deviate from the data beyond the rho peak as can be seen in figure
1.3

Figure 1.3:
Pion form factor for
s=q^{2}>0

This model has also been used to compute the electromagnetic radius of the pion with
{\left\langle{r_{\pi}^{2}}\right\rangle_{S}}=0.40\penalty\ \text{fm}^{2}
in
[
22
]
.

There are limitations to the KLZ model. The KLZ only provides a partial description of the pion-rho interactions. The particle content is only of the charged pions and neutral rho. There are more pion-rho interactions in nature than are captured by the KLZ, which ignores the neutral pion and charged rhos thus excluding a description of the decay of the charged rhos into pions. To address some of these limitations in the KLZ we shall have to chart a new path forward. The new model must use a larger gauge group to accommodate the full complement of the triplet of pions and rhos.

As an application of the new model we have chosen to calculate the pion-pion scattering lengths. The pion-pion scattering amplitude has been studied extensively through the Roy equations and Chiral Pertubartion theory
[
23
,
24
,
25
,
26
]
. Experimentally, NA48/2
[
27
]
and DIRAC
[
28
]
have collected large data sets which allow for the precise extraction of the scattering lengths.

Outline

This document is organized in the following way. In part I, chapter 2, we develop the bare SU(2) model. Chapter 3 is dedicated to Spontaneous symmetry breaking to generate the rho mass. In chapter 4, we quantize the new Lagrangian, introduce the Faddeev-Popov ghosts and fix the gauge. Chapter 5 contains the renormalization transformation and Feynman rules. In Part II, we apply the new model to evaluating the pion-pion scattering lengths. Chapter 6 has the calculations of the tree scattering lengths and results. In chapter 7, we start the program of finding the one loop corrections to the tree scattering lengths. Due to time constraints on a PhD project, the numerical evaluations have been excluded. Finally chapter 8 summarizes with a conclusion.

Part I
Generalizing the KLZ

2
Extending the KLZ

2.1
SU(2) Generalization

𝕎
e will now proceed in generalizing the U(1) KLZ model. What are some simple expectations could we have of such a model? We would like a model describing the interactions of pseudoscalar pions and vector rhos which includes the full triplet of pions
\{\pi^{-},\pi^{0},\pi^{+}\}
and the triplet of rhos
\{\rho^{-},\rho^{0},\rho^{+}\}
. We would like it to be renormalizable since it would have some predictive power when considering higher order corrections. We shall make some simplifying assumptions. We shall assume all the particles are point like. We shall ignore the difference between the masses of the charged and neutral pion
(m_{\pi^{\pm}}-m_{\pi^{0}}=4.5936\pm 0.0005\penalty\ \rm{MeV})

[
29
]
. This small difference (small in comparison to their mass) in pion masses is due to the mass difference between the u and d quarks. This approximate flavour symmetry becomes exact in the chiral limit and we have the isospin SU(2) symmetry group. The pions are then in the adjoint representation of SU(2). We also ignore the difference in rho masses
(m_{\rho^{\pm}}-m_{\rho^{0}}=0.7\pm 0.8\penalty\ \rm{MeV})

[
29
]
.

With these assumptions we can build an effective description of the interactions between the pions and rhos. The rhos will play the role of dynamical gauge
bosons
[
30
]
mediating the interactions between the pions and the rhos themselves. We will let the principle of local gauge invariance guide our construction under which only terms invariant under SU(2) gauge transformations are to be admitted into our Lagrangian. We begin by writing down a SU(2) globally gauge invariant Lagrangian for scalars representing the pions [
\Phi(x):=\phi^{a}=\{\phi^{1},\phi^{2},\phi^{3}\}
]

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}=\frac{1}{2}\left[{\left({{\partial_{\mu}}\Phi}\right)^{\dagger}}{\partial^{\mu}}\Phi-b^{2}{\Phi^{\dagger}}\Phi\right]

(2.1.1)

where
b
has dimensions of mass. The Lagrangian (
2.1.1
) is invariant under the unitary transformation of

\displaystyle U

\displaystyle=e^{iT^{a}\alpha^{a}}\in\rm{SU(2)}

(2.1.2)

\displaystyle\Phi

\displaystyle\to\Phi^{\prime}=U\Phi

(2.1.3)

where
T^{a}
are the generators of the group and are elements of the Lie algebra
T^{a}\in\mathfrak{su(2)}
and
\alpha^{a}\in\mathbb{R}
are some constants. The repeated
a
’s imply a sum over
a=\{1,2,3\}
. When we promote the global gauge transformation to a local one with
{\alpha^{a}}\to{\alpha^{a}}\left(x\right)
, the derivatives in the Lagrangian produce extra terms which breaks the gauge invariance of the Lagrangian. The extra terms are proportional to the generators
T^{a}
, so to remedy the problem we will introduce an extra field into the partial derivative as

\displaystyle{\partial_{\mu}}\to{D_{\mu}}:={\partial_{\mu}}+ie{T^{a}}A_{\mu}^{a}(x)

(2.1.4)

Here
D_{\mu}
is the gauge covariant derivative,
e
the gauge coupling which is dimensionless,
[e]=1
and
A_{\mu}^{a}(x)
are the gauge fields representing the rhos. The gauge fields are in the adjoint representation of SU(2) so we expanded them using the generators as a basis. The covariant derivative is built to transform such that when we make a unitary transformation on the field
\Phi
, the gauge fields
A_{\mu}^{a}
must also transform to keep the Lagrangian invariant. This is done by insisting that

\displaystyle D{{}^{\prime}_{\mu}}\Phi^{\prime}=U{D_{\mu}}\Phi

(2.1.5)

which together with (
2.1.3
) gives us the transformation rule for the covariant derivative as

\displaystyle{D_{\mu}}\to D{{}^{\prime}_{\mu}}=U{D_{\mu}}{U^{\dagger}}

(2.1.6)

We can infer the transformation rule for the gauge fields from the above condition as

\displaystyle D{{}^{\prime}_{\mu}}f

\displaystyle=U{D_{\mu}}{U^{\dagger}}f

\displaystyle\left({{\partial_{\mu}}+ieA{{}^{\prime}_{\mu}}}\right)f

\displaystyle=U\left({{\partial_{\mu}}+ie{A_{\mu}}}\right){U^{\dagger}}f

\displaystyle\Rightarrow A{{}^{\prime}_{\mu}}

\displaystyle=UA_{\mu}{U^{\dagger}}+\frac{1}{{ie}}U{\partial_{\mu}}{U^{\dagger}}

(2.1.7)

with
A{{}^{\prime}}_{\mu}=A{{}^{\prime}}_{\mu}^{a}T^{a}
and
f
was some arbitrary function included to keep track of the derivatives. Now when we make a unitary transformation on the fields and noting that

\displaystyle\Phi\to

\displaystyle\Phi^{\prime}=U\Phi

(2.1.8)

\displaystyle{D_{\mu}}\to

\displaystyle D{{}^{\prime}_{\mu}}=U{D_{\mu}}{U^{\dagger}}

(2.1.9)

then the Lagrangian

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}=\frac{1}{2}\left[{\left({{D_{\mu}}\Phi}\right)^{\dagger}}{D^{\mu}}\Phi-b^{2}{\Phi^{\dagger}}\Phi\right]

(2.1.10)

remains invariant. Under the unitary transformation of the scalar field, the gauge field was required to maintain the gauge invariance of
the Lagrangian. The gauge fields are just passive auxiliary fields at this stage. If the gauge fields are to represent the rhos then they
must have some dynamic behaviour. So naturally we would like to make this a requirement for the gauge fields. Though since this is a
nonabelian theory, we must take care when we construct the kinetic term for the gauge fields. For a kinetic term we require a scalar,
which is Lorentz and gauge invariant, and quadratic in the first derivatives of the field. The commutator serves as
natural product so we shall construct an operator from the commutator of the covariant derivatives,

\displaystyle{{\hat{O}}_{\mu\nu}}f=\left[{{D_{\mu}},{D_{\nu}}}\right]f

(2.1.11)

where
f
is some arbitrary function we included to keep track of the derivatives. Under SU(2) gauge transformations, the operator changes as
{{\hat{O}}_{\mu\nu}}\to\hat{O}{{}^{\prime}_{\mu\nu}}

\displaystyle\hat{O}{{}^{\prime}_{\mu\nu}}f

\displaystyle=\left[{D{{}^{\prime}_{\mu}},D{{}^{\prime}_{\nu}}}\right]f

\displaystyle=\left[{U{D_{\mu}}{U^{\dagger}},U{D_{\nu}}{U^{\dagger}}}\right]f

\displaystyle=\left({U{D_{\mu}}{D_{\nu}}{U^{\dagger}}-U{D_{\nu}}{D_{\mu}}{U^{\dagger}}}\right)f

\displaystyle=U\left[{{D_{\mu}},{D_{\nu}}}\right]{U^{\dagger}}f

\displaystyle=U{{\hat{O}}_{\mu\nu}}{U^{\dagger}}f

(2.1.12)

So we see that the transformed operator is,

\displaystyle\hat{O}{{}^{\prime}_{\mu\nu}}=U{{\hat{O}}_{\mu\nu}}{U^{\dagger}}

(2.1.13)

We need to ensure that a kinetic term is Lorentz and gauge invariant. We can create a new operator by squaring
O^{\mu\nu}
:

\displaystyle\hat{O}{{}^{\prime}_{\mu\nu}}\hat{O}{{}^{\prime\mu\nu}}

\displaystyle=U{{\hat{O}}_{\mu\nu}}{U^{\dagger}}U{{\hat{O}}^{\mu\nu}}{U^{\dagger}}

\displaystyle=U{{\hat{O}}_{\mu\nu}}{{\hat{O}}^{\mu\nu}}{U^{\dagger}}

(2.1.14)

Looking at (
2.1.14
) we can see that this operator is Lorentz invariant but still dependent on the SU(2) gauge transformation
U
. We also have to deal with (
2.1.14
) not being a scalar, since only scalar functions enter into the action. We have to transform (
2.1.14
) into a scalar while preserving its properties. We can remove the gauge dependence and create a scalar function by taking the trace of (
2.1.14
) as

\displaystyle{\rm{Tr}}\left[{\hat{O}{{}^{\prime}_{\mu\nu}}\hat{O}{{}^{\prime\mu\nu}}}\right]

\displaystyle={\rm{Tr}}\left[{U{{\hat{O}}_{\mu\nu}}{{\hat{O}}^{\mu\nu}}{U^{\dagger}}}\right]

\displaystyle={\rm{Tr}}\left[{{{\hat{O}}_{\mu\nu}}{{\hat{O}}^{\mu\nu}}U{U^{\dagger}}}\right]

\displaystyle={\rm{Tr}}\left[{{{\hat{O}}_{\mu\nu}}{{\hat{O}}^{\mu\nu}}}\right]

(2.1.15)

which is independent of the gauge transformation. Now since this operator (
2.1.15
) is the square of the first derivatives of the fields and invariant under Lorentz and gauge transformations, it has the necessary behaviour of a kinetic term of the gauge fields. We will define the nonabelian field strength tensor as

\displaystyle{G_{\mu\nu}}:=\frac{1}{ie}{{\hat{O}}_{\mu\nu}}=\frac{1}{ie}\left[{{D_{\mu}},{D_{\nu}}}\right]

(2.1.16)

We can get the explicit form of the tensor by substituting in the covariant derivatives into (
2.1.16
), where
f
is included to keep track of the derivatives.

\displaystyle{G_{\mu\nu}}f

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{{ie}}\left[{{D_{\mu}},{D_{\nu}}}\right]f

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{{ie}}\left[{{\partial_{\mu}}+ie{A_{\mu}},{\partial_{\nu}}+ie{A_{\nu}}}\right]f

\displaystyle=\biggl({{\partial_{\mu}}{A_{\nu}}-{\partial_{\nu}}{A_{\mu}}+ie\left[{{A_{\mu}},{A_{\nu}}}\right]}\biggr)f

(2.1.17)

So the field strength tensor is

\displaystyle{G_{\mu\nu}}={\partial_{\mu}}{A_{\nu}}-{\partial_{\nu}}{A_{\mu}}+ie\left[{{A_{\mu}},{A_{\nu}}}\right]

(2.1.18)

We can use the elements of
\rm\mathfrak{su(2)}
to write
{G_{\mu\nu}}
as a sum over the generators. Using the Lie algebra

\displaystyle\left[{{T^{b}},{T^{c}}}\right]=i{\varepsilon_{abc}}{T^{a}}

(2.1.19)

where
\varepsilon_{abc}
is the Levi-Civita symbol representing the structure constants of the algebra, and
A_{\mu}=A^{a}_{\mu}T^{a}
, we get the following expression

\displaystyle G_{\mu\nu}^{a}{T^{a}}

\displaystyle={\partial_{\mu}}A_{\nu}^{a}{T^{a}}-{\partial_{\nu}}A_{\mu}^{a}{T^{a}}+ie\left[{A_{\mu}^{b}{T^{b}},A_{\nu}^{c}{T^{c}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\left[{{\partial_{\mu}}A_{\nu}^{a}-{\partial_{\nu}}A_{\mu}^{a}-e{\varepsilon_{abc}}A_{\mu}^{b}A_{\nu}^{c}}\right]{T^{a}}

(2.1.20)

From (
2.1.20
) we can recognise the familiar abelian field strength tensor in the first two terms in (
2.1.20
). We will define

\displaystyle F_{\mu\nu}^{a}:={\partial_{\mu}}A_{\nu}^{a}-{\partial_{\nu}}A_{\mu}^{a}

(2.1.21)

then the nonabelian field strength becomes

\displaystyle G_{\mu\nu}^{a}=F_{\mu\nu}^{a}-e{\varepsilon_{abc}}A_{\mu}^{b}A_{\nu}^{c}

(2.1.22)

We can now define in analogy to (
2.1.15
) a kinetic term for the gauge fields as (the Yang-Mills term)

\displaystyle{\mathscr{L}_{YM}}

\displaystyle:=-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{Tr}\left[{{G_{\mu\nu}}{G^{\mu\nu}}}\right]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}G^{a}_{\mu\nu}G^{b\mu\nu}\mathrm{Tr}\left[{{T^{a}}{T^{b}}}\right]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4}G^{a}_{\mu\nu}G^{a\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4}F_{\mu\nu}^{a}{F^{a\mu\nu}}+\frac{1}{2}e{\varepsilon_{abc}}{A^{b\mu}}{A^{c\nu}}F_{\mu\nu}^{a}-\frac{1}{4}{e^{2}}{\varepsilon_{abc}}{\varepsilon_{ade}}A_{\mu}^{b}A_{\nu}^{c}{A^{d\mu}}{A^{e\nu}}

(2.1.23)

where
-\dfrac{1}{2}
is a factor by convention and we have used the normalization condition

\displaystyle\rm{Tr}\left[{{T^{a}}{T^{b}}}\right]=\frac{1}{2}{\delta^{ab}}

(2.1.24)

We should pause at this stage and interpret what each term in (
2.1.23
) represents. After all we went in search for a kinetic term for the gauge field, but requiring Lorentz and local gauge invariance provided extra terms. So the first term in (
2.1.23
) is the pure kinetic term, the second and third terms represent three point and four point interactions among the gauge fields.
So the full nonabelian Lagrangian is

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left[{\left({{D_{\mu}}\Phi}\right)^{\dagger}}{D^{\mu}}\Phi-b^{2}{\Phi^{\dagger}}\Phi\right]-\frac{1}{4}{G^{a}}_{\mu\nu}{G^{a}}^{\mu\nu}

(2.1.25)

Expanding out the covariant derivatives

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}{\left({{D_{\mu}}\Phi}\right)^{\dagger}}{D^{\mu}}\Phi=\frac{1}{2}\left\{{{{\left({{\partial_{\mu}}\Phi}\right)}^{\dagger}}{\partial^{\mu}}\Phi+ie\left[{{{\left({{\partial_{\mu}}\Phi}\right)}^{\dagger}}{A^{\mu}}\Phi-{\Phi^{\dagger}}{A^{\mu}}{\partial_{\mu}}\Phi}\right]+{e^{2}}{\Phi^{\dagger}}{A^{\mu}}{A_{\mu}}\Phi}\right\}

(2.1.26)

we can write out all the terms in components of the fields using the adjoint representation of the generators
{\left({{T^{a}}}\right)_{ij}}=-i{\varepsilon_{aij}}
, so the terms become

\displaystyle{\left({{\partial^{\mu}}\Phi}\right)^{\dagger}}{A_{\mu}}\Phi

\displaystyle=A_{\mu}^{a}{\left({{\partial^{\mu}}\Phi}\right)^{\dagger}}{T_{a}}\Phi

\displaystyle=A_{\mu}^{a}{\left({{\partial^{\mu}}{\phi_{i}}}\right)^{\dagger}}{\left({{T^{a}}}\right)_{ij}}{\phi_{j}}

\displaystyle=-i{\varepsilon_{aij}}A_{\mu}^{a}\left({{\partial^{\mu}}{\phi_{i}}}\right){\phi_{j}}

\displaystyle\implies{\Phi^{\dagger}}{A_{\mu}}\left({{\partial^{\mu}}\Phi}\right)

\displaystyle={\left[{{{\left({{\partial^{\mu}}\Phi}\right)}^{\dagger}}{A_{\mu}}\Phi}\right]^{\dagger}}

\displaystyle=-i{\varepsilon_{aij}}A_{\mu}^{a}{\phi_{i}}\left({{\partial^{\mu}}{\phi_{j}}}\right)

with

\displaystyle{\left({{\partial^{\mu}}\Phi}\right)^{\dagger}}{A_{\mu}}\Phi-{\Phi^{\dagger}}{A_{\mu}}\left({{\partial^{\mu}}\Phi}\right)

\displaystyle=-iA_{\mu}^{a}{\varepsilon_{aij}}\left[{{\phi_{j}}\left({{\partial^{\mu}}{\phi_{i}}}\right)-{\phi_{i}}\left({{\partial^{\mu}}{\phi_{j}}}\right)}\right]

(2.1.27)

and

\displaystyle{\Phi^{\dagger}}{A^{\mu}}{A_{\mu}}\Phi

\displaystyle=A_{\mu}^{a}{A^{b\mu}}{\phi_{i}}{\left({{T^{a}}}\right)_{ij}}{\left({{T^{b}}}\right)_{jk}}{\phi_{k}}

\displaystyle=-{\varepsilon_{aij}}{\varepsilon_{bjk}}{\phi_{i}}{\phi_{k}}A_{\mu}^{a}{A^{b\mu}}

(2.1.28)

So the Lagrangian in component form is

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left({{\partial_{\mu}}{\phi_{a}}{\partial^{\mu}}{\phi_{a}}-{b^{2}}\phi_{a}^{2}}\right)+\frac{1}{2}e{\varepsilon_{aij}}A_{\mu}^{a}\left({{\phi_{j}}{\partial^{\mu}}{\phi_{i}}-{\phi_{i}}{\partial^{\mu}}{\phi_{j}}}\right)-\frac{1}{2}{e^{2}}{\varepsilon_{aij}}{\varepsilon_{bjk}}{\phi_{i}}{\phi_{k}}A_{\mu}^{a}{A^{b\mu}}-\frac{1}{4}F_{\mu\nu}^{a}{F^{a\mu\nu}}+

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}e{\varepsilon_{abc}}{A^{b\mu}}{A^{c\nu}}F_{\mu\nu}^{a}-\frac{1}{4}{e^{2}}{\varepsilon_{abc}}{\varepsilon_{ade}}A_{\mu}^{b}A_{\nu}^{c}{A^{d\mu}}{A^{e\nu}}

(2.1.29)

Now there has been an omission from the start, we claimed that under the principle of local gauge invariance all terms which preserve the invariance of the Lagrangian are valid terms for inclusion into the Lagrangian. This allows for the inclusion of a polynomial with an infinite number of terms of the form

\displaystyle P(\Phi)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{n=1}^{\infty}c_{n}(\Phi^{\dagger}\Phi)^{n}

\displaystyle=b^{2}\Phi^{\dagger}\Phi+\frac{\lambda_{4}}{8}(\Phi^{\dagger}\Phi)^{2}+\sum\limits_{n=3}^{\infty}c_{n}(\Phi^{\dagger}\Phi)^{n}

(2.1.30)

The first term we included from the start with
b
having dimensions of mass,
[b]=M
, the second term represents a self coupling and
\lambda_{4}
is dimensionless,
[\lambda_{4}]=1
. This quartic coupling term for the pions was left out in the original U(1) KLZ formulation. There is no legitimate reason at this stage to exclude this term so we shall include it in the Lagrangian. For terms greater than
n=3
, the coefficients
c_{n}
have dimensions of inverse mass,
[c_{n}]=M^{-n}
. This poses a problem to our requirement for a renormalizable theory
[
31
]
. We shall thus ignore all terms for
n\geq 3
. Then finally the Lagrangian is

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{\phi A}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left({{\partial_{\mu}}{\phi_{a}}{\partial^{\mu}}{\phi_{a}}-{b^{2}}\phi_{a}^{2}}\right)+\frac{1}{2}e{\varepsilon_{aij}}A_{\mu}^{a}\left({{\phi_{j}}{\partial^{\mu}}{\phi_{i}}-{\phi_{i}}{\partial^{\mu}}{\phi_{j}}}\right)-\frac{1}{2}{e^{2}}{\varepsilon_{aij}}{\varepsilon_{bjk}}{\phi_{i}}{\phi_{k}}A_{\mu}^{a}{A^{b\mu}}-\frac{{{\lambda_{4}}}}{8}\left({\phi_{a}^{2}\phi_{b}^{2}}\right)+

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}F_{\mu\nu}^{a}{F^{a\mu\nu}}+\frac{1}{2}e{\varepsilon_{abc}}{A^{b\mu}}{A^{c\nu}}F_{\mu\nu}^{a}-\frac{1}{4}{e^{2}}{\varepsilon_{abc}}{\varepsilon_{ade}}A_{\mu}^{b}A_{\nu}^{c}{A^{d\mu}}{A^{e\nu}}

(2.1.31)

Let us provide an interpretation for the remaining terms in (
2.1.31
). The first two terms in the parenthesis behave like the kinetic and mass-like
1
1

1

These are not the couplings nor physical mass identified from experiment. Since we have not renormalized and completed the definition of the theory as yet these terms only have the dimensions of a mass and appearance of couplings.
terms of a scalar field. The third and fourth terms are the three and four point interaction terms between the scalar and vector fields. Note of course the lack of a mass-like term for the gauge field.

3
Spontaneous Symmetry Breaking

3.1
Higgs Mechanism

𝔸
s noted in the last chapter the Lagrangian has a mass term for the pions but none for the rhos. For this quantum field theory to be a good description of reality, it would be wise to endow the particles with the appropriate masses. The term
b^{2}\Phi^{\dagger}\Phi
for the pion was included by hand since the form of the term remained invariant under gauge transformations. We could attempt to do the same for the gauge field by including a term proportional to the form
A^{\mu}A_{\mu}
, but recalling the transformation rule for the gauge field in (
2.1.7
) shown here below

\displaystyle A{{}^{\prime}_{\mu}}

\displaystyle=UA_{\mu}{U^{\dagger}}+\frac{1}{{ie}}U{\partial_{\mu}}{U^{\dagger}}

We see that the product is

\displaystyle A{{}^{\prime}_{\mu}}A{{}^{\prime\mu}}=U{A_{\mu}}{A^{\mu}}{U^{\dagger}}+\frac{1}{{ie}}\left[{U{A_{\mu}}{\partial^{\mu}}{U^{\dagger}}+U\left({{\partial^{\mu}}{U^{\dagger}}}\right)U{A_{\mu}}{U^{\dagger}}}\right]-\frac{1}{{{e^{2}}}}U\left({{\partial^{\mu}}{U^{\dagger}}}\right)U\left({{\partial^{\mu}}{U^{\dagger}}}\right)

(3.1.1)

This does not look very appealing, we could use the identity
U^{\dagger}U=1

\displaystyle{\partial^{\mu}}\left({{U^{\dagger}}U}\right)

\displaystyle=0\Rightarrow{U^{\dagger}}\left({{\partial^{\mu}}U}\right)=-\left({{\partial^{\mu}}{U^{\dagger}}}\right)U

(3.1.2)

\displaystyle{\partial^{\mu}}\left({U{U^{\dagger}}}\right)

\displaystyle=0\Rightarrow U\left({{\partial^{\mu}}{U^{\dagger}}}\right)=-\left({{\partial^{\mu}}U}\right){U^{\dagger}}

(3.1.3)

and write the product as

\displaystyle A{{}^{\prime}_{\mu}}A{{}^{\prime\mu}}=U{A_{\mu}}{A^{\mu}}{U^{\dagger}}-\frac{1}{{ie}}U\left[{{A_{\mu}}{U^{\dagger}}{\partial^{\mu}}U+{U^{\dagger}}\left({{\partial^{\mu}}U}\right){A_{\mu}}}\right]{U^{\dagger}}+\frac{1}{{{e^{2}}}}{\partial^{\mu}}U{\partial^{\mu}}{U^{\dagger}}

(3.1.4)

We could argue that this expression is not a scalar, and for the case of the Yang-Mills kinetic term it was necessary to take the trace to yield a scalar which also removed the gauge dependence. Unfortunately this does not work for a mass term as

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\left[{A{{}^{\prime}_{\mu}}A{{}^{\prime\mu}}}\right]=\mathrm{Tr}\left[{{A_{\mu}}{A^{\mu}}}\right]-\frac{2}{{ie}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[{A_{\mu}{U^{\dagger}}{\partial^{\mu}}U}\right]+\frac{1}{{{e^{2}}}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[{{\partial^{\mu}}U^{\dagger}{\partial^{\mu}}{U}}\right]

(3.1.5)

and (
3.1.5
) still has remnants of the gauge transformation. So we cannot put in a mass term for the gauge field by hand without breaking gauge invariance. The mass term for the gauge field must be generated dynamically
[
32
]
. This is done by introducing a new scalar field which has rotational symmetry with respect to some potential. A translation in the field is performed and the previous rotational symmetry becomes hidden. This process is referred to as the Higgs mechanism or spontaneous symmetry breaking. We note that this is not the Higgs mechanism used in the Standard Model to give all the elementary particles their masses. We use a Higgs-like mechanism here only to give mass to the rhos. Generating the vector mass using the Higgs mechanism preserves the renormalizability of the theory
[
33
]
.

We introduce a new field
X
which is a complex doublet that has the gauge transformation

\displaystyle X\to X^{\prime}=UX

(3.1.6)

We construct an SU(2) gauge invariant Higgs Lagrangian, with the covariant derivatives acting on the complex doublets

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}=\left(D_{\mu}X\right)^{\dagger}\left(D^{\mu}X\right)-V\left(X,X^{\dagger}\right)-\kappa\left(X^{\dagger}X\right)\left(\Phi^{\dagger}\Phi\right)

(3.1.7)

where the potential
V\left(X,X^{\dagger}\right)
is

\displaystyle V\left(X,X^{\dagger}\right)=\dfrac{\lambda}{8}\left(X^{\dagger}X\right)^{2}-\dfrac{\mu^{2}}{2}\left(X^{\dagger}X\right)

(3.1.8)

which has been tuned for symmetry breaking. The term
\kappa\left(X^{\dagger}X\right)\left(\Phi^{\dagger}\Phi\right)
is present since it is gauge invariant and thus allowed by the principle of local gauge invariance.
\kappa
is the coupling between the pions and complex doublet and is dimensionless,
[\kappa]=1
. Plotting
V\left(X,X^{\dagger}\right)
we can see the rotational symmetry present in the potential [under rotations about an axis through
(0,0)
] by the contours which are circles.

Figure 3.1:
Rotationally symmetric symmetry breaking potential.

We can express the potential in terms of real fields by decomposing the complex double into a doublet of real valued fields.

\displaystyle X=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}x_{2}+ix_{1}\\
x_{0}-ix_{3}\end{pmatrix}

(3.1.9)

so

\displaystyle X^{\dagger}X

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{2}x_{\alpha}x_{\alpha}

(3.1.10)

\displaystyle\left(X^{\dagger}X\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{4}x_{\alpha}x_{\alpha}x_{\beta}x_{\beta}

(3.1.11)

We note the usage of the repeated Greek index in the above expressions. We only resort to using the Greek index since the sum is over
\{0,1,2,3\}
. The potential in terms of the real fields is

\displaystyle V\left(x_{\alpha}\right)=\dfrac{\lambda}{32}x_{\alpha}x_{\alpha}x_{\beta}x_{\beta}-\dfrac{\mu^{2}}{4}x_{\alpha}x_{\alpha}

(3.1.12)

We now search for the minimum (the vacuum) of this potential.

\displaystyle\dfrac{\partial V}{\partial x_{\kappa}}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\lambda}{32}\left(2x_{\alpha}\delta_{\alpha\kappa}x^{2}_{\beta}+x^{2}_{\alpha}\cdot 2x_{\beta}\delta_{\beta\kappa}\right)-\dfrac{\mu^{2}}{4}\cdot 2x_{\alpha}\delta_{\alpha\kappa}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\lambda}{32}\left(4x_{\kappa}x^{2}_{\alpha}\right)-\dfrac{\mu^{2}}{2}x_{\kappa}

\displaystyle=x_{\kappa}\left(\dfrac{\lambda}{8}x^{2}_{\alpha}-\dfrac{\mu^{2}}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=0

which has solutions

\displaystyle x_{\kappa}=0\qquad\text{OR}\qquad x^{2}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=4\dfrac{\mu^{2}}{\lambda}

(3.1.13)

Computing the second derivative to test the above solutions

\displaystyle\frac{{{\partial^{2}}V}}{{\partial{x_{\pi}}\partial{x_{\kappa}}}}={\delta_{\kappa\pi}}\left({\frac{\lambda}{8}x_{\alpha}^{2}-\frac{{{\mu^{2}}}}{2}}\right)+\frac{\lambda}{8}{x_{\kappa}}{x_{\pi}}

with the results along the axes
\kappa=\pi

\displaystyle{\left.{\dfrac{{{\partial^{2}}V}}{{\partial{x_{\pi}}\partial{x_{\kappa}}}}}\right|_{{x_{\alpha}=0}}}=-\frac{{{\mu^{2}}}}{2}\leq 0,\qquad{\left.{\dfrac{{{\partial^{2}}V}}{{\partial{x_{\pi}}\partial{x_{\kappa}}}}}\right|_{x_{\alpha}^{2}=4\frac{{{\mu^{2}}}}{\lambda}}}={\mu^{2}}\geq 0

The
x_{\kappa}=0
yields a local maximum for the potential and the
x^{2}_{\alpha}=4\dfrac{\mu^{2}}{\lambda}
solution results in the local minimum. We can see these solutions from the plot in figure
3.1
. There is a circle of solutions which are the minima of the potential. We define

\displaystyle\nu^{2}:=\dfrac{\mu^{2}}{\lambda}\qquad\chi^{2}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=4\nu^{2}

(3.1.14)

We shall pick one of the possible minima with

\displaystyle x_{1}=x_{2}=x_{3}=0\qquad\text{AND}\qquad x_{0}=2\nu

(3.1.15)

The
X
field has acquired its vacuum expectation value with the above choice of a minimum. The vacuum is located at

\displaystyle X_{\text{M}}=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
2\nu\end{pmatrix}

(3.1.16)

Defining a new field
\chi

\displaystyle\chi:=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}\chi_{2}+i\chi_{1}\\
\chi_{0}-i\chi_{3}\end{pmatrix}

(3.1.17)

we now consider fluctuations about the minimum
X_{\text{M}}
by defining

\displaystyle X:=\chi+X_{M}

(3.1.18)

This is just translating the vacuum to the origin.
Now we rewrite the Lagrangian in terms of this new translated field. Starting with the potential

\displaystyle X^{\dagger}X

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{2}\left(\chi_{\alpha}\chi_{\alpha}+4\nu\chi_{0}+4\nu^{2}\right)

(3.1.19)

\displaystyle\left(X^{\dagger}X\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{4}\left[\chi_{\alpha}\chi_{\alpha}\chi_{\beta}\chi_{\beta}+8\nu\chi_{0}\chi_{\alpha}\chi_{\alpha}+16\nu^{2}\chi^{2}_{0}+8\nu^{2}\chi_{\alpha}\chi_{\alpha}+32\nu^{3}\chi_{0}+16\nu^{4}\right]

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{4}\chi^{2}_{\alpha}\chi^{2}_{\beta}+2\nu\chi_{0}\chi^{2}_{\alpha}+4\nu^{2}\chi^{2}_{0}+2\nu^{2}\chi^{2}_{\alpha}+8\nu^{3}\chi_{0}+4\nu^{4}

(3.1.20)

So the potential is given by

\displaystyle V\left({{\chi_{\alpha}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda}{8}{\left({{X^{\dagger}}X}\right)^{2}}-\frac{{{\mu^{2}}}}{2}{X^{\dagger}}X

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda}{{32}}\chi_{\alpha}^{2}\chi_{\beta}^{2}+\frac{{\nu\lambda}}{4}{\chi_{0}}\chi_{\beta}^{2}+\frac{{{\mu^{2}}}}{2}\chi_{0}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}{\mu^{2}}{\nu^{2}}

(3.1.21)

The pion-chi interaction becomes

\displaystyle\left({{X^{\dagger}}X}\right)\left({{\Phi^{\dagger}}\Phi}\right)=2{\nu^{2}}\phi_{a}^{2}+2\nu{\chi_{0}}\phi_{a}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\chi_{\alpha}^{2}\phi_{a}^{2}

The covariant derivatives expanded out is

\displaystyle{\left({{D_{\mu}}X}\right)^{\dagger}}{D^{\mu}}X

\displaystyle={\partial_{\mu}}{X^{\dagger}}{\partial^{\mu}}X+ie\left({{\partial^{\mu}}{X^{\dagger}}{A_{\mu}}X-{X^{\dagger}}{A_{\mu}}{\partial^{\mu}}X}\right)+{e^{2}}{X^{\dagger}}{A^{\mu}}{A_{\mu}}X

(3.1.22)

The kinetic term of the Lagrangian is then

\displaystyle{\partial_{\mu}}{X^{\dagger}}{\partial^{\mu}}X

\displaystyle={\partial_{\mu}}{\chi^{\dagger}}{\partial^{\mu}}\chi

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{{\sqrt{2}}}\begin{pmatrix}{{\partial_{\mu}}{\chi_{2}}-i{\partial_{\mu}}{\chi_{1}}}&{{\partial_{\mu}}{\chi_{0}}+i{\partial_{\mu}}{\chi_{3}}}\end{pmatrix}\frac{1}{{\sqrt{2}}}\begin{pmatrix}{{\partial_{\mu}}{\chi_{2}}+i{\partial_{\mu}}{\chi_{1}}}\\
{{\partial_{\mu}}{\chi_{0}}-i{\partial_{\mu}}{\chi_{3}}}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}{\partial_{\mu}}{\chi_{\alpha}}{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi_{\alpha}}

(3.1.23)

We note that the gauge field expanded over the generators are

\displaystyle{A_{\mu}}

\displaystyle=A_{\mu}^{a}{T^{a}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}{A_{\mu}^{3}}&{A_{\mu}^{1}-iA_{\mu}^{2}}\\
{A_{\mu}^{1}+iA_{\mu}^{2}}&{-A_{\mu}^{3}}\end{pmatrix}

(3.1.24)

\displaystyle\implies{A_{\mu}}{A^{\mu}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\begin{pmatrix}{A_{\mu}^{a}{A^{a\mu}}}&0\\
0&{A_{\mu}^{a}{A^{a\mu}}}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}A_{\mu}^{a}{A^{a\mu}}{\mathbb{1}_{2\times 2}}

(3.1.25)

The four field coupling term of (
3.1.22
) can be expressed using the above expression as

\displaystyle{X^{\dagger}}{A^{\mu}}{A_{\mu}}X

\displaystyle={X^{\dagger}}\frac{1}{4}A_{\mu}^{a}{A^{a\mu}}{\mathbb{1}_{2\times 2}}X

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}{\nu^{2}}A_{\mu}^{a}{A^{a\mu}}+\frac{1}{2}\nu{\chi_{0}}A_{\mu}^{a}{A^{a\mu}}+\frac{1}{8}\chi_{\alpha}^{2}A_{\mu}^{a}{A^{a\mu}}

(3.1.26)

The process of picking a vacuum and translating it to the origin, thus hiding the rotational symmetry has generated a mass. The first term in the above equation represents a mass for the gauge field. Lastly we have to deal with the three field coupling term of (
3.1.22
).

\displaystyle{\partial^{\mu}}{X^{\dagger}}{A_{\mu}}X

\displaystyle={\partial^{\mu}}{\chi^{\dagger}}{A_{\mu}}{X_{\rm{M}}}+{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi^{\dagger}}{A_{\mu}}\chi

\displaystyle=-{\chi^{\dagger}}{\partial^{\mu}}{A_{\mu}}{X_{\rm{M}}}+{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi^{\dagger}}{A_{\mu}}\chi

(3.1.27)

We have integrated by parts (using the integral over the Lagrangian) and discarded the surface term for the first term of the above expression. Substituting the above expression into the three field coupling term

\displaystyle{\partial^{\mu}}{X^{\dagger}}{A_{\mu}}X-{X^{\dagger}}{A_{\mu}}{\partial^{\mu}}X

\displaystyle=X_{\rm{M}}^{\dagger}{\partial^{\mu}}{A_{\mu}}\chi-{\chi^{\dagger}}{\partial^{\mu}}{A_{\mu}}{X_{\rm{M}}}+{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi^{\dagger}}{A_{\mu}}\chi-{\chi^{\dagger}}{A_{\mu}}{\partial^{\mu}}\chi

\displaystyle={\partial^{\mu}}A_{\mu}^{a}\left[{X_{\rm{M}}^{\dagger}{T^{a}}\chi-{\chi^{\dagger}}{T^{a}}{X_{\rm{M}}}}\right]+{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi^{\dagger}}{A_{\mu}}\chi-{\chi^{\dagger}}{A_{\mu}}{\partial^{\mu}}\chi

(3.1.28)

we see a separation into two types of interaction terms. The last two terms are three field interaction terms but the term in the
square brackets seems to have two field interaction terms i.e. mixing terms. Let us check

\displaystyle{\chi^{\dagger}}{\partial^{\mu}}{A_{\mu}}X_{\rm{M}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{{\sqrt{2}}}\begin{pmatrix}{{\chi_{2}}-i{\chi_{1}}}&{{\chi_{0}}+i{\chi_{3}}}\end{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2}{\partial^{\mu}}\begin{pmatrix}{A_{\mu}^{3}}&{A_{\mu}^{1}-iA_{\mu}^{2}}\\
{A_{\mu}^{1}+iA_{\mu}^{2}}&{-A_{\mu}^{3}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{{2\sqrt{2}}}\left[{\left({{\chi_{2}}{\partial^{\mu}}A_{\mu}^{1}-{\chi_{1}}{\partial^{\mu}}A_{\mu}^{2}-{\chi_{0}}{\partial^{\mu}}A_{\mu}^{3}}\right)-i\left({{\chi_{2}}{\partial^{\mu}}A_{\mu}^{2}+{\chi_{1}}{\partial^{\mu}}A_{\mu}^{1}+{\chi_{3}}{\partial^{\mu}}A_{\mu}^{3}}\right)}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{{2\sqrt{2}}}\left[{-i{\chi_{a}}{\partial^{\mu}}A_{\mu}^{a}+\left({{\chi_{2}}{\partial^{\mu}}A_{\mu}^{1}-{\chi_{1}}{\partial^{\mu}}A_{\mu}^{2}-{\chi_{0}}{\partial^{\mu}}A_{\mu}^{3}}\right)}\right]

(3.1.29)

Taking the hermitian conjugate of (
3.1.29
) and substituting into the two field interaction terms of (
3.1.28
) we get

\displaystyle X_{\rm{M}}^{\dagger}{\partial^{\mu}}{A_{\mu}}\chi-{\chi^{\dagger}}{\partial^{\mu}}{A_{\mu}}{X_{\rm{M}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{{\sqrt{2}}}{\chi_{a}}{\partial^{\mu}}A_{\mu}^{a}

(3.1.30)

which indeed has only two fields interacting a point. These pesky terms are hard to interpret so we will leave them alone for now and come back to them when we discuss gauge fixing. The remaining term to work on is the three field interaction terms of (
3.1.28
). First the
{A_{\mu}}\chi
product

\displaystyle{A_{\mu}}\chi

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}{A_{\mu}^{3}}&{A_{\mu}^{1}-iA_{\mu}^{2}}\\
{A_{\mu}^{1}+iA_{\mu}^{2}}&{-A_{\mu}^{3}}\end{pmatrix}\frac{1}{{\sqrt{2}}}\begin{pmatrix}{{\chi_{2}}+i{\chi_{1}}}\\
{{\chi_{0}}-i{\chi_{3}}}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{{2\sqrt{2}}}\begin{pmatrix}{A_{\mu}^{3}{\chi_{2}}+A_{\mu}^{1}{\chi_{0}}-A_{\mu}^{2}{\chi_{3}}+i\left({A_{\mu}^{3}{\chi_{1}}-A_{\mu}^{1}{\chi_{3}}-A_{\mu}^{2}{\chi_{0}}}\right)}\\
{A_{\mu}^{1}{\chi_{2}}+A_{\mu}^{2}{\chi_{1}}-A_{\mu}^{3}{\chi_{0}}+i\left({A_{\mu}^{1}{\chi_{1}}+A_{\mu}^{2}{\chi_{2}}+A_{\mu}^{3}{\chi_{3}}}\right)}\end{pmatrix}

(3.1.31)

then the three field interaction term is

\displaystyle{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi^{\dagger}}{A_{\mu}}\chi

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{{\sqrt{2}}}\begin{pmatrix}{{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi_{2}}-i{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi_{1}}}&{{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi_{0}}+i{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi_{3}}}\end{pmatrix}\frac{1}{{2\sqrt{2}}}\begin{pmatrix}{A_{\mu}^{3}{\chi_{2}}+A_{\mu}^{1}{\chi_{0}}-A_{\mu}^{2}{\chi_{3}}+i\left({A_{\mu}^{3}{\chi_{1}}-A_{\mu}^{1}{\chi_{3}}-A_{\mu}^{2}{\chi_{0}}}\right)}\\
{A_{\mu}^{1}{\chi_{2}}+A_{\mu}^{2}{\chi_{1}}-A_{\mu}^{3}{\chi_{0}}+i\left({A_{\mu}^{1}{\chi_{1}}+A_{\mu}^{2}{\chi_{2}}+A_{\mu}^{3}{\chi_{3}}}\right)}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\bigg\{\left({A_{\mu}^{3}{\chi_{2}}+A_{\mu}^{1}{\chi_{0}}-A_{\mu}^{2}{\chi_{3}}}\right){\partial^{\mu}}{\chi_{2}}+\left({A_{\mu}^{3}{\chi_{1}}-A_{\mu}^{1}{\chi_{3}}-A_{\mu}^{2}{\chi_{0}}}\right){\partial^{\mu}}{\chi_{1}}+

\displaystyle+i\left[{{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi_{2}}\left({A_{\mu}^{3}{\chi_{1}}-A_{\mu}^{1}{\chi_{3}}-A_{\mu}^{2}{\chi_{0}}}\right)-\left({A_{\mu}^{3}{\chi_{2}}+A_{\mu}^{1}{\chi_{0}}-A_{\mu}^{2}{\chi_{3}}}\right){\partial^{\mu}}{\chi_{1}}}\right]+

\displaystyle+\left({A_{\mu}^{1}{\chi_{2}}+A_{\mu}^{2}{\chi_{1}}-A_{\mu}^{3}{\chi_{0}}}\right){\partial^{\mu}}{\chi_{0}}-\left({A_{\mu}^{1}{\chi_{1}}+A_{\mu}^{2}{\chi_{2}}+A_{\mu}^{3}{\chi_{3}}}\right){\partial^{\mu}}{\chi_{3}}+

\displaystyle+i\left[{{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi_{0}}\left({A_{\mu}^{1}{\chi_{1}}+A_{\mu}^{2}{\chi_{2}}+A_{\mu}^{3}{\chi_{3}}}\right)+\left({A_{\mu}^{1}{\chi_{2}}+A_{\mu}^{2}{\chi_{1}}-A_{\mu}^{3}{\chi_{0}}}\right){\partial^{\mu}}{\chi_{3}}}\right]\bigg\}

(3.1.32)

The appearance of the individual
\chi_{\alpha}
fields interacting differently is troubling. Taking the hermitian conjugate of the above and substituting into the three field interaction terms of (
3.1.28
)

\displaystyle{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi^{\dagger}}{A_{\mu}}\chi-{\chi^{\dagger}}{A_{\mu}}{\partial^{\mu}}\chi

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{2}\bigg[\left({\chi_{1}}{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi_{2}}-{\chi_{2}}{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi_{1}}\right)A_{\mu}^{3}+\left({\chi_{3}}{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi_{0}}-{\chi_{0}}{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi_{3}}\right)A_{\mu}^{3}+

\displaystyle+\,\,\,\quad\left({{\chi_{2}}{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi_{3}}-{\chi_{3}}{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi_{2}}}\right)A_{\mu}^{1}+\left({{\chi_{1}}{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi_{0}}-{\chi_{0}}{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi_{1}}}\right)A_{\mu}^{1}+

\displaystyle+\,\,\,\quad\left({{\chi_{2}}{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi_{0}}-{\chi_{0}}{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi_{2}}}\right)A_{\mu}^{2}+\left({{\chi_{3}}{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi_{1}}-{\chi_{1}}{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi_{3}}}\right)A_{\mu}^{2}\bigg]

(3.1.33)

A pattern emerges in the way the individual
\chi_{\alpha}
fields interact with the gauge fields. To clarify we define the operator

\displaystyle A\overleftrightarrow{\partial^{\mu}}B:=A{\partial^{\mu}}B-B{\partial^{\mu}}A

(3.1.34)

Rewriting (
3.1.33
) in terms of the above operator

\displaystyle{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi^{\dagger}}{A_{\mu}}\chi-{\chi^{\dagger}}{A_{\mu}}{\partial^{\mu}}\chi

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{2}\bigg[\left({\chi_{1}}\overleftrightarrow{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi_{2}}\right)A_{\mu}^{3}+\left({\chi_{3}}\overleftrightarrow{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi_{0}}\right)A_{\mu}^{3}+

\displaystyle+\,\,\,\quad\left({\chi_{2}}\overleftrightarrow{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi_{3}}\right)A_{\mu}^{1}+\left({\chi_{1}}\overleftrightarrow{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi_{0}}\right)A_{\mu}^{1}+

\displaystyle+\,\,\,\quad\left({\chi_{2}}\overleftrightarrow{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi_{0}}\right)A_{\mu}^{2}+\left({\chi_{3}}\overleftrightarrow{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi_{1}}\right)A_{\mu}^{2}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{2}\left[{\frac{1}{2}{\varepsilon_{abc}}\left({{\chi_{a}}\overleftrightarrow{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi_{b}}}\right)A_{\mu}^{c}+\left({{\chi_{a}}\overleftrightarrow{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi_{0}}}\right)A_{\mu}^{a}}\right]

(3.1.35)

The pattern for the
\chi_{\alpha}
fields interacting allowed us to write the three field interactions compactly in terms of the
Levi-Civita symbol, with the
\chi_{1},\chi_{2},\chi_{3}
interacting among themselves with the gauge fields and the separate interaction term
with the
\chi_{0}
field. We have all the needed pieces, substituting (
3.1.30
) and (
3.1.35
) into
(
3.1.28
)

\displaystyle{\partial^{\mu}}{X^{\dagger}}{A_{\mu}}X-{X^{\dagger}}{A_{\mu}}{\partial^{\mu}}X

\displaystyle={\partial^{\mu}}A_{\mu}^{a}\left[{X_{\rm{M}}^{\dagger}{T^{a}}\chi-{\chi^{\dagger}}{T^{a}}{X_{\rm{M}}}}\right]+{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi^{\dagger}}{A_{\mu}}\chi-{\chi^{\dagger}}{A_{\mu}}{\partial^{\mu}}\chi

\displaystyle={\partial^{\mu}}A_{\mu}^{a}\left[{X_{\rm{M}}^{\dagger}{T^{a}}\chi-{\chi^{\dagger}}{T^{a}}{X_{\rm{M}}}}\right]+\frac{i}{2}\left[{\frac{1}{2}{\varepsilon_{abc}}\left({{\chi_{a}}\overleftrightarrow{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi_{b}}}\right)A_{\mu}^{c}+\left({{\chi_{a}}\overleftrightarrow{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi_{0}}}\right)A_{\mu}^{a}}\right]

(3.1.36)

The covariant derivative in terms of the real
\chi_{\alpha}
fields are

\displaystyle{\left({{D_{\mu}}X}\right)^{\dagger}}{D^{\mu}}X

\displaystyle={\partial_{\mu}}{\chi^{\dagger}}{\partial^{\mu}}\chi+ie\left({{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi^{\dagger}}{A_{\mu}}\chi-{\chi^{\dagger}}{A_{\mu}}{\partial^{\mu}}\chi}\right)+{e^{2}}{\chi^{\dagger}}{A^{\mu}}{A_{\mu}}\chi

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}{\partial_{\mu}}{\chi_{\alpha}}{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi_{\alpha}}+ie{\partial^{\mu}}A_{\mu}^{a}\left[{X_{\rm{M}}^{\dagger}{T^{a}}\chi-{\chi^{\dagger}}{T^{a}}{X_{\rm{M}}}}\right]-\frac{1}{4}e{\varepsilon_{abc}}\left({{\chi_{a}}\overleftrightarrow{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi_{b}}}\right)A_{\mu}^{c}-\frac{1}{2}e\left({{\chi_{a}}\overleftrightarrow{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi_{0}}}\right)A_{\mu}^{a}+

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}{e^{2}}{\nu^{2}}A_{\mu}^{a}{A^{a\mu}}+\frac{1}{2}{e^{2}}\nu{\chi_{0}}A_{\mu}^{a}{A^{a\mu}}+\frac{1}{8}{e^{2}}\chi_{\alpha}^{2}A_{\mu}^{a}{A^{a\mu}}

(3.1.37)

Substituting in the covariant derivative, the potential and the
X^{\dagger}X\Phi^{\dagger}\Phi
interaction terms into the Lagrangian after symmetry breaking

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{H}

\displaystyle={\left({{D_{\mu}}X}\right)^{\dagger}}{D^{\mu}}X-V\left(X,X^{\dagger}\right)-\kappa\left({{X^{\dagger}}X}\right)\left({{\Phi^{\dagger}}\Phi}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left({{\partial_{\mu}}{\chi_{0}}{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi_{0}}-{\mu^{2}}\chi_{0}^{2}}\right)+\frac{1}{2}{\partial_{\mu}}{\chi_{a}}{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi_{a}}+ie{\partial^{\mu}}A_{\mu}^{a}\left[{X_{\rm{M}}^{\dagger}{T^{a}}\chi-{\chi^{\dagger}}{T^{a}}{X_{\rm{M}}}}\right]-\frac{1}{4}e{\varepsilon_{abc}}\left({{\chi_{a}}\overleftrightarrow{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi_{b}}}\right)A_{\mu}^{c}+

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}e\left({{\chi_{a}}\overleftrightarrow{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi_{0}}}\right)A_{\mu}^{a}+\frac{1}{2}{e^{2}}{\nu^{2}}A_{\mu}^{a}{A^{a\mu}}+\frac{1}{2}{e^{2}}\nu{\chi_{0}}A_{\mu}^{a}{A^{a\mu}}+\frac{1}{8}{e^{2}}\chi_{\alpha}^{2}A_{\mu}^{a}{A^{a\mu}}-\frac{\lambda}{{32}}\chi_{\alpha}^{2}\chi_{\beta}^{2}-\frac{{\nu\lambda}}{4}{\chi_{0}}\chi_{\alpha}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\left({4\kappa{\nu^{2}}}\right){\phi^{2}_{a}}+

\displaystyle-2\kappa\nu{\chi_{0}}{\phi^{2}_{a}}-\frac{1}{2}\kappa\chi_{\alpha}^{2}{\phi^{2}_{a}}+\frac{1}{2}{\mu^{2}}{\nu^{2}}

(3.1.38)

Due to our choice of the vacuum along the
\chi_{0}
direction, the
\chi_{0}
field behaves differently from the
\chi_{1},\chi_{2},\chi_{3}
terms. We will separate the
\chi_{0}
component out with

\displaystyle\chi_{\alpha}^{2}

\displaystyle=\chi_{0}^{2}+\chi_{a}^{2}

(3.1.39)

\displaystyle\chi_{\alpha}^{2}\chi_{\beta}^{2}

\displaystyle=\chi_{0}^{4}+2\chi_{0}^{2}\chi_{a}^{2}+\chi_{a}^{2}\chi_{b}^{2}

and make a cosmetic change in relabeling the
\chi_{0}
component with
{\chi_{0}}:=H
which we shall refer to for convenience as the Higgs field. A reminder again, this is not the Standard Model Higgs field.

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{H}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left({{\partial_{\mu}}H{\partial^{\mu}}H-{\mu^{2}}{H^{2}}}\right)+\frac{1}{2}{\partial_{\mu}}{\chi_{a}}{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi_{a}}+ie{\partial^{\mu}}A_{\mu}^{a}\left[{X_{\rm{M}}^{\dagger}{T^{a}}\chi-{\chi^{\dagger}}{T^{a}}{X_{\rm{M}}}}\right]-\frac{1}{4}e{\varepsilon_{abc}}\left({{\chi_{a}}\overleftrightarrow{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi_{b}}}\right)A_{\mu}^{c}+

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}e\left({{\chi_{a}}\overleftrightarrow{\partial^{\mu}}H}\right)A_{\mu}^{a}+\frac{1}{2}{e^{2}}{\nu^{2}}A_{\mu}^{a}{A^{a\mu}}+\frac{1}{2}{e^{2}}\nu HA_{\mu}^{a}{A^{a\mu}}+\frac{1}{8}{e^{2}}{H^{2}}A_{\mu}^{a}{A^{a\mu}}+\frac{1}{8}{e^{2}}\chi_{b}^{2}A_{\mu}^{a}{A^{a\mu}}-\frac{\lambda}{{32}}{H^{4}}-\frac{\lambda}{{16}}{H^{2}}\chi_{a}^{2}+

\displaystyle-\frac{\lambda}{{32}}\chi_{a}^{2}\chi_{b}^{2}-\frac{{\nu\lambda}}{4}{H^{3}}-\frac{{\nu\lambda}}{4}H\chi_{a}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\left({4\kappa{\nu^{2}}}\right){\phi^{2}_{a}}-2\kappa\nu H{\phi^{2}_{a}}-\frac{1}{2}\kappa{H^{2}}{\phi^{2}_{a}}-\frac{1}{2}\kappa\chi_{a}^{2}{\phi^{2}_{a}}+\frac{1}{2}{\mu^{2}}{\nu^{2}}

(3.1.40)

Some terms of (
3.1.40
) require some special attention, we point out the entire process of spontaneous symmetry breaking was
to generate a mass for the gauge field. This was achieved with the generation of the term
\frac{1}{2}{e^{2}}{\nu^{2}}A_{\mu}^{a}{A^{a\mu}}
.
A contribution to the mass of the pion was generated during the symmetry breaking process, this depended on the coupling
\kappa
as

\frac{1}{2}\left({4\kappa{\nu^{2}}}\right){\phi^{2}_{a}}
. A mass term for the Higgs field was also generated during this process with
the mass term being
\frac{1}{2}{\mu^{2}}{H^{2}}
. The
\chi_{a}
are massless and are the three Goldstone fields. The rotational symmetry is
still present in the Lagrangian, it is hidden from casual inspection. We could reverse the process and translate the potential back to
its original configuration. The rotational symmetry would be made explicit again. We noted the appearance of the pesky mixing terms which
are present in the above Lagrangian.

4
Quantization

4.1
Faddeev-Popov Ghosts

𝕋
he classical Lagrangian is ready for quantization. There is an obvious problem at
the start. Since this is a gauge theory, there are
infinitely many field configurations which are related to each other via a gauge transformation which led to equivalent states. This
leads to an over-counting of the physical states and must be dealt with as it leads to an overall multiplicative divergence. We can
extract out the contribution from the physically distinct states via a suitable gauge fixing process. This means that the gauge
transformation partitions the configuration space of gauge fields and sets up an equivalence class for sets of physically equivalent
gauge fields. This leads to a problem when summing over all field contributions as there are infinitely many physically equivalent
field configurations leading to a divergence. So we need to count only one member from each partition once and this is done with a gauge
fixing function which is designed to traverse the configuration space and intersect the set of all physically equivalent gauge fields only once. This is the method pioneered by Feynman and formalized by Faddeev and Popov
[
34
]
. Consider a
function
g(x)
and some
w\in\mathbb{R}
with

\displaystyle g(x)=w

(4.1.1)

which has roots at
x_{k}
which can be ordered as
{x_{1}}<{x_{2}}<\cdots<{x_{N}}
and
g^{\prime}\left({{x_{i}}}\right)\neq 0
, then

\displaystyle\int{dx\delta\left[{g\left(x\right)-w}\right]}=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{N}{\frac{1}{{\left|{g^{\prime}\left({{x_{k}}}\right)}\right|}}}

(4.1.2)

we have the one dimensional identity

\displaystyle{\left[{\sum\limits_{k=1}^{N}{\frac{1}{{\left|{g^{\prime}\left({{x_{k}}}\right)}\right|}}}}\right]^{-1}}\int{dx\delta\left[{g\left(x\right)-w}\right]}=1

(4.1.3)

We restrict the class of functions
g(x)
to those which yield only a single root and this simplifies to

\displaystyle{\left|{\frac{d}{{dx}}g\left(x\right)}\right|_{x=a}}\int{dx\delta\left[{g\left(x\right)-w}\right]}=1

(4.1.4)

This can be generalized to the field theoretic version

\displaystyle\Delta\left[A\right]\int{\mathcal{D}\mu[U]\delta\left[{{G^{a}}\left({{A^{U}}}\right)-{w^{a}}}\right]}=1

(4.1.5)

where
\Delta\left[A\right]
is a generalization of the derivative of the function
g(x)
, and will later turn out to be a
Jacobian matrix.
A^{U}
indicates the dependence of the gauge field on the transformation
U
.
\mathcal{D}\mu[U]
is the Haar
measure
[
31
]
. The condition placed on
g(x)
earlier was to select injective mappings as candidates for gauge fixing functions
{G^{a}}\left({{A^{U}}}\right)
. This is the problem of the Gribov Ambiguity
[
35
,
36
]
. For perturbative considerations we will stay within one horizon.

Consider now the path integral for the gauge field

\displaystyle Z^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\int{\mathcal{D}A{e^{iS\left[A\right]}}}

\displaystyle=\int{\mathcal{D}{A^{U}}{e^{iS\left[{{A^{U}}}\right]}}\cdot 1}

\displaystyle=\int{\mathcal{D}{A^{U}}{e^{iS\left[{{A^{U}}}\right]}}\Delta\left[{{A^{U}}}\right]\int{\mathcal{D}\mu[U]\delta\left[{{G^{a}}\left({{A^{U}}}\right)-{w^{a}}}\right]}}

(4.1.6)

We have inserted a
1
using the identity (
4.1.5
). We can now make a gauge transformation where
{A^{U}}\to A
and noting the Haar measure is invariant under gauge transformations

\displaystyle Z^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\int{\mathcal{D}A{e^{iS\left[A\right]}}\Delta\left[A\right]\int{\mathcal{D}\mu[U]\delta\left[{{G^{a}}\left(A\right)-{w^{a}}}\right]}}

\displaystyle=\int{\mathcal{D}\mu[U]}\int{\mathcal{D}A{e^{iS\left[A\right]}}\Delta\left[A\right]\delta\left[{{G^{a}}\left(A\right)-{w^{a}}\left(x\right)}\right]}

We can insert a
1
in the form of a ratio of Gaussian functionals and absorb the denominator into the measure.

\displaystyle Z^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\int{\mathcal{D}A{e^{iS\left[A\right]}}\Delta\left[A\right]\int{\mathcal{D}\mu[U]\delta\left[{{G^{a}}\left(A\right)-{w^{a}}}\right]}}

\displaystyle=\int{\mathcal{D}\mu[U]}\dfrac{{\int{\mathcal{D}w{e^{-\frac{i}{{2\xi}}\int{{d^{4}}x}w_{a}^{2}\left(x\right)}}}}}{{\int{\mathcal{D}w{e^{-\frac{i}{{2\xi}}\int{{d^{4}}x}w_{a}^{2}\left(x\right)}}}}}\int{\mathcal{D}A{e^{iS\left[A\right]}}\Delta\left[A\right]\delta\left[{{G^{a}}\left(A\right)-{w^{a}}\left(x\right)}\right]}

\displaystyle=\int{\mathcal{D}\widetilde{\mu}[U]}\int{\mathcal{D}w{e^{-\frac{i}{{2\xi}}\int{{d^{4}}x}w_{a}^{2}\left(x\right)}}}\int{\mathcal{D}A{e^{iS\left[A\right]}}\Delta\left[A\right]\delta\left[{{G^{a}}\left(A\right)-{w^{a}}\left(x\right)}\right]}

(4.1.7)

Since all the fields are independent of the gauge transformation we can factor out the integral over the measure which contributes an overall multiplicative divergence. Note also that
Z^{\prime}
can not depend on the parameter
\xi
, since only a
1
was inserted.

\displaystyle\frac{{Z^{\prime}}}{{\int{\mathcal{D}\widetilde{\mu}[U]}}}=\int{\mathcal{D}A\mathcal{D}w{e^{iS\left[A\right]}}{e^{-\frac{i}{{2\xi}}\int{{d^{4}}x}w_{a}^{2}\left(x\right)}}\Delta\left[A\right]\delta\left[{{G^{a}}\left(A\right)-{w^{a}}\left(x\right)}\right]}

(4.1.8)

We can now define the path integral which does not suffer from the over-counting

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}

\displaystyle:=\frac{{Z^{\prime}}}{{\int{\mathcal{D}\widetilde{\mu}[U]}}}

\displaystyle=\int{\mathcal{D}A\mathcal{D}w{e^{iS\left[A\right]}}{e^{-\frac{i}{{2\xi}}\int{{d^{4}}x}w_{a}^{2}\left(x\right)}}\Delta\left[A\right]\delta\left[{{G^{a}}\left(A\right)-{w^{a}}\left(x\right)}\right]}

\displaystyle=\int{\mathcal{D}A{e^{iS\left[A\right]}}{e^{-\frac{i}{{2\xi}}\int{{d^{4}}x}G_{a}^{2}\left(A\right)}}\Delta\left[A\right]}

(4.1.9)

where we have integrated over the
w
using the delta functional. We are left with the task of evaluating
\Delta[A]
. Recall the identity
in (
4.1.5
)

\Delta\left[A\right]\int{\mathcal{D}\mu[U]\delta\left[{{G^{a}}\left({{A^{U}}}\right)-{w^{a}}}\right]}=1

with the transformation
U={e^{i{\alpha^{a}}{T^{a}}}}
, we see that the invariant Haar measure
\mathcal{D}\mu[U]\propto\mathcal{D}\alpha
. We can rewrite the identity as

\displaystyle\Delta\left[A\right]\int{\mathcal{D}\alpha\delta\left[{{G^{a}}\left({{}^{\alpha}A}\right)-{w^{a}}}\right]}=1

(4.1.10)

We note the change in notation from
A^{\alpha}
(which looks like the components of a Lorentz vector) to
{}^{\alpha}A
(which serves to indicate the dependence of the gauge field on the group parameters
\alpha
). We can make a change of variables

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}G

\displaystyle=\det\left[{\frac{{\delta{G^{b}}\left[{{}^{\alpha}A;x}\right]}}{{\delta{\alpha^{c}}\left(y\right)}}}\right]\mathcal{D}\alpha

\displaystyle\implies\mathcal{D}\alpha

\displaystyle=\mathcal{D}G\cdot\frac{1}{{\det\left[{\frac{{\delta{G^{b}}\left[{{}^{\alpha}A;x}\right]}}{{\delta{\alpha^{c}}\left(y\right)}}}\right]}}

(4.1.11)

Substituting in the new measure

\displaystyle\Delta\left[A\right]\int{\mathcal{D}G\cdot\dfrac{1}{{\det\left[{\frac{{\delta{G^{b}}\left[{{}^{\alpha}A;x}\right]}}{{\delta{\alpha^{c}}\left(y\right)}}}\right]}}\delta\left[{{G^{b}}\left({{}^{\alpha}A}\right)-{w^{b}}}\right]}

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle\Delta\left[A\right]\left.\dfrac{1}{\det{\left[{\dfrac{{\delta{G^{b}}\left[{{}^{\alpha}A;x}\right]}}{{\delta{\alpha^{c}}\left(y\right)}}}\right]}}\right|_{{G^{b}}\left({{}^{\alpha}A}\right)={w^{b}}}

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle\Delta\left[A\right]

\displaystyle=\det{\left.{\left[{\frac{{\delta{G^{b}}\left[{{}^{\alpha}A;x}\right]}}{{\delta{\alpha^{c}}\left(y\right)}}}\right]}\right|_{{G^{b}}\left({{}^{\alpha}A}\right)={w^{b}}}}

\displaystyle=\det{M^{bc}}\left({x,y}\right)

(4.1.12)

We observe that
\Delta[A]
is the determinant of a matrix of variational derivatives of the gauge fixing function with respect to the group parameters and we defined

\displaystyle{M^{bc}}\left({x,y}\right)

\displaystyle:=\frac{{\delta{G^{b}}\left[{{}^{\alpha}A;x}\right]}}{{\delta{\alpha^{c}}\left(y\right)}}

(4.1.13)

We can choose a class of gauge fixing functions
[
37
,
38
]

\displaystyle{G^{b}}\left[{{}^{\alpha}A;x}\right]={\partial^{\mu}}A_{\mu}^{b}+\xi ie\left({X_{\rm{M}}^{\dagger}{T^{b}}\chi-{\chi^{\dagger}}{T^{b}}X_{\rm{M}}}\right)

(4.1.14)

We shall justify the choice of (
4.1.14
) in the next section. The derivative of the gauge fixing function with respect to the group parameters is

\displaystyle\frac{{\delta{G^{b}}\left[{{}^{\alpha}A;x}\right]}}{{\delta{\alpha^{c}}\left(y\right)}}

\displaystyle=\int{{d^{4}}z\left[{\frac{\delta}{{\delta A_{\nu}^{d}\left(z\right)}}{\partial^{\mu}}A_{\mu}^{b}\left(x\right)}\right]\frac{{\delta A_{\nu}^{d}\left(z\right)}}{{\delta{\alpha^{c}}\left(y\right)}}}+\frac{\delta}{{\delta{\alpha^{c}}\left(y\right)}}\xi ie\left({X_{\rm{M}}^{\dagger}{T^{b}}\chi-{\chi^{\dagger}}{T^{b}}X_{\rm{M}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\int{{d^{4}}z\partial_{x}^{\mu}{\delta_{bd}}\delta\left({x-z}\right)g_{\mu}^{\nu}\frac{{\delta A_{\nu}^{d}\left(z\right)}}{{\delta{\alpha^{c}}\left(y\right)}}}+\frac{\delta}{{\delta{\alpha^{c}}\left(y\right)}}\xi ie\left({X_{\rm{M}}^{\dagger}{T^{b}}\chi-{\chi^{\dagger}}{T^{b}}X_{\rm{M}}}\right)

(4.1.15)

We now turn to finding the variation of the gauge field with respect to the group parameters. Recall that the gauge field transforms as

\displaystyle{{A^{\prime}}_{\mu}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{{ie}}U{\partial_{\mu}}{U^{\dagger}}+U{A_{\mu}}{U^{\dagger}}

\displaystyle U

\displaystyle={e^{i{\alpha^{a}}{T^{a}}}}

(4.1.16)

Since we are looking for the variational derivative we need only concern ourselves with working with the infinitesimal transformation, so for
\delta{\alpha_{a}}\ll 1

\displaystyle U

\displaystyle\simeq 1+i\delta{\alpha^{a}}{T^{a}}+\mathcal{O}\left({\delta\alpha_{a}^{2}}\right)

(4.1.17)

The gauge field transforms as

\displaystyle{{A^{\prime}}_{\mu}}

\displaystyle\simeq\frac{1}{{ie}}\left({1+i\delta{\alpha^{a}}{T^{a}}}\right){\partial_{\mu}}\left({1-i\delta{\alpha^{b}}{T^{b}}}\right)+\left({1+i\delta{\alpha^{a}}{T^{a}}}\right){A_{\mu}}\left({1-i\delta{\alpha^{b}}{T^{b}}}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left({\delta\alpha_{a}^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{e}\left({1+i\delta{\alpha^{a}}{T^{a}}}\right){\partial_{\mu}}\delta{\alpha^{b}}{T^{b}}+\left({1+i\delta{\alpha^{a}}{T^{a}}}\right)\left({{A_{\mu}}-i\delta{\alpha^{b}}{A_{\mu}}{T^{b}}}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left({\delta\alpha_{a}^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle={A_{\mu}}-\frac{1}{e}{\partial_{\mu}}\delta{\alpha^{a}}{T^{a}}-i\delta{\alpha^{a}}A_{\mu}^{b}{T^{b}}{T^{a}}+i\delta{\alpha^{a}}{T^{a}}A_{\mu}^{b}{T^{b}}+\mathcal{O}\left({\delta\alpha_{a}^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle={A_{\mu}}-\frac{1}{e}{\partial_{\mu}}\delta{\alpha^{a}}{\delta^{ac}}{T^{c}}+i\delta{\alpha^{a}}A_{\mu}^{b}\left[{{T^{a}},{T^{b}}}\right]+\mathcal{O}\left({\delta\alpha_{a}^{2}}\right)

(4.1.18)

Using the algebra of the group
\left[{{T^{a}},{T^{b}}}\right]=i{\varepsilon_{abc}}{T^{c}}
.

\displaystyle{{A^{\prime}}_{\mu}}

\displaystyle={A_{\mu}}-\frac{1}{e}{\partial_{\mu}}\delta{\alpha^{a}}{\delta^{ac}}{T^{c}}-{\varepsilon_{abc}}{T^{c}}\delta{\alpha^{a}}A_{\mu}^{b}+\mathcal{O}\left({\delta\alpha_{a}^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle={A_{\mu}}-\frac{1}{e}\left[{{\delta^{ac}}{\partial_{\mu}}+e{\varepsilon_{abc}}A_{\mu}^{b}}\right]\delta{\alpha^{a}}{T^{c}}+\mathcal{O}\left({\delta\alpha_{a}^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle={A_{\mu}}-\frac{1}{e}D_{\mu}^{ac}\delta{\alpha^{a}}{T^{c}}+\mathcal{O}\left({\delta\alpha_{a}^{2}}\right)

(4.1.19)

We recognise the covariant derivative
D_{\mu}^{ac}
in the adjoint representation. Expanding the gauge field over the generators

\displaystyle A^{\prime c}_{\mu}{T^{c}}=A_{\mu}^{c}{T^{c}}-\frac{1}{e}D_{\mu}^{ac}\delta{\alpha^{a}}{T^{c}}+\mathcal{O}\left({\delta\alpha_{a}^{2}}\right)

(4.1.20)

Extracting only the components

\displaystyle A^{\prime c}_{\mu}

\displaystyle=A_{\mu}^{c}-\frac{1}{e}D_{\mu}^{ac}\delta{\alpha^{a}}+\mathcal{O}\left({\delta\alpha_{a}^{2}}\right)

(4.1.21)

\displaystyle\delta A_{\nu}^{d}\left(z\right)

\displaystyle:=A^{\prime d}_{\nu}\left(z\right)-A_{\nu}^{d}\left(z\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{e}D_{\nu}^{ad}\delta{\alpha^{a}}\left(z\right)+\mathcal{O}\left({\delta\alpha_{\nu}^{2}}\right)

(4.1.22)

We can now construct the variational derivative,

\displaystyle\dfrac{{\delta A_{\nu}^{d}\left(z\right)}}{{\delta{\alpha^{c}}\left(y\right)}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{e}D_{\nu}^{ad}\frac{{\delta{\alpha^{a}}\left(z\right)}}{{\delta{\alpha^{c}}\left(y\right)}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{e}D_{\nu}^{ad}{\delta^{ac}}\delta\left({z-y}\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{e}D_{\nu}^{cd}\delta\left({z-y}\right)

(4.1.23)

Next is the variation of the Higgs and Goldstone fields. Recall the definition of the translated field in (
3.1.18
)
from symmetry breaking shown below

\displaystyle X:=\chi+{X_{\rm{M}}}

Under a gauge transformation this transforms
[
39
,
40
]
as

\displaystyle X^{\prime}=\chi^{\prime}+{X_{\rm{M}}}

(4.1.24)

with
X^{\prime}=UX
. So we can deduce the transformation rule for the translated field
\chi^{\prime}

\displaystyle\chi^{\prime}

\displaystyle=UX-{X_{\rm{M}}}

\displaystyle=U\chi+\left({U-1}\right){X_{\rm{M}}}

(4.1.25)

For infinitesimal transformations

\displaystyle\delta\chi

\displaystyle:=\chi^{\prime}-\chi

\displaystyle=i\delta{\alpha^{c}}{T^{c}}\left({\chi+{X_{\rm{M}}}}\right)

(4.1.26)

The variation of the Higgs and Goldstone fields are

\displaystyle\delta\left({X_{\rm{M}}^{\dagger}{T^{b}}\chi-{\chi^{\dagger}}{T^{b}}{X_{\rm{M}}}}\right)

\displaystyle=X_{\rm{M}}^{\dagger}{T^{b}}\delta\chi-\delta{\chi^{\dagger}}{T^{b}}{X_{\rm{M}}}

\displaystyle=i\delta{\alpha^{c}}\left[{X_{\rm{M}}^{\dagger}{T^{b}}{T^{c}}\left({\chi+{X_{\rm{M}}}}\right)+\left({{\chi^{\dagger}}+X_{\rm{M}}^{\dagger}}\right){T^{c}}{T^{b}}{X_{\rm{M}}}}\right]

\displaystyle=i\delta{\alpha^{c}}\left[{X_{\rm{M}}^{\dagger}{T^{b}}{T^{c}}{X_{\rm{M}}}+X_{\rm{M}}^{\dagger}{T^{c}}{T^{b}}{X_{\rm{M}}}+X_{\rm{M}}^{\dagger}{T^{b}}{T^{c}}\chi+{\chi^{\dagger}}{T^{c}}{T^{b}}{X_{\rm{M}}}}\right]

\displaystyle=i\delta{\alpha^{c}}\left[{X_{\rm{M}}^{\dagger}\left\{{{T^{b}},{T^{c}}}\right\}{X_{\rm{M}}}+X_{\rm{M}}^{\dagger}{T^{b}}{T^{c}}\chi+{\chi^{\dagger}}{T^{c}}{T^{b}}{X_{\rm{M}}}}\right]

(4.1.27)

We can use
{T^{b}}=\frac{1}{2}{\sigma^{b}}
and the anti-commutation relations

\displaystyle\left\{{{T^{b}},{T^{c}}}\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\left\{{{\sigma^{b}},{\sigma^{c}}}\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}{\delta^{bc}}

(4.1.28)

and recalling the location of the vacuum from (
3.1.16
) shown below

\displaystyle X_{\text{M}}=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
2\nu\end{pmatrix}\implies X_{\text{M}}^{\dagger}X_{\text{M}}=2{\nu^{2}}

the first term of (
4.1.27
) can be simplified to

\displaystyle X_{\rm{M}}^{\dagger}\left\{{{T^{b}},{T^{c}}}\right\}{X_{\rm{M}}}={\nu^{2}}{\delta^{bc}}

(4.1.29)

We now turn to evaluating the last two terms of (
4.1.27
). We show two ways to calculate this which serves also as an algebraic check. First, the brute force way is to note the sigma matrices can be parameterized as

\displaystyle{\sigma^{b}}=\begin{pmatrix}{{\delta_{b3}}}&{{\delta_{b1}}-i{\delta_{b2}}}\\
{{\delta_{b1}}+i{\delta_{b2}}}&{-{\delta_{b3}}}\end{pmatrix}

(4.1.30)

and the product of the sigma matrices is

\displaystyle{\sigma^{c}}{\sigma^{b}}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}{{\delta_{ci}}{\delta_{bi}}+i\left({{\delta_{c1}}{\delta_{b2}}-{\delta_{c2}}{\delta_{b1}}}\right)}&{{\delta_{c3}}{\delta_{b1}}-{\delta_{c1}}{\delta_{b3}}+i\left({{\delta_{c2}}{\delta_{b3}}-{\delta_{c3}}{\delta_{b2}}}\right)}\\
{{\delta_{c1}}{\delta_{b3}}-{\delta_{c3}}{\delta_{b1}}+i\left({{\delta_{c2}}{\delta_{b3}}-{\delta_{c3}}{\delta_{b2}}}\right)}&{{\delta_{ci}}{\delta_{bi}}+i\left({{\delta_{c2}}{\delta_{b1}}-{\delta_{c1}}{\delta_{b2}}}\right)}\end{pmatrix}

(4.1.31)

This product of generators acting on the vacuum

\displaystyle{T^{c}}{T^{b}}X_{\rm{M}}

\displaystyle=\frac{{2\nu}}{{4\sqrt{2}}}{\sigma^{c}}{\sigma^{b}}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\frac{\nu}{{2\sqrt{2}}}\begin{pmatrix}{{\delta_{ci}}{\delta_{bi}}+i\left({{\delta_{c1}}{\delta_{b2}}-{\delta_{c2}}{\delta_{b1}}}\right)}&{{\delta_{c3}}{\delta_{b1}}-{\delta_{c1}}{\delta_{b3}}+i\left({{\delta_{c2}}{\delta_{b3}}-{\delta_{c3}}{\delta_{b2}}}\right)}\\
{{\delta_{c1}}{\delta_{b3}}-{\delta_{c3}}{\delta_{b1}}+i\left({{\delta_{c2}}{\delta_{b3}}-{\delta_{c3}}{\delta_{b2}}}\right)}&{{\delta_{ci}}{\delta_{bi}}+i\left({{\delta_{c2}}{\delta_{b1}}-{\delta_{c1}}{\delta_{b2}}}\right)}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\frac{\nu}{{2\sqrt{2}}}\begin{pmatrix}{{\delta_{c3}}{\delta_{b1}}-{\delta_{c1}}{\delta_{b3}}+i\left({{\delta_{c2}}{\delta_{b3}}-{\delta_{c3}}{\delta_{b2}}}\right)}\\
{{\delta_{ci}}{\delta_{bi}}+i\left({{\delta_{c2}}{\delta_{b1}}-{\delta_{c1}}{\delta_{b2}}}\right)}\end{pmatrix}

(4.1.32)

Recalling the translated field
\chi

\displaystyle\chi=\frac{1}{{\sqrt{2}}}\begin{pmatrix}{{\chi_{2}}+i{\chi_{1}}}\\
{{\chi_{0}}-i{\chi_{3}}}\end{pmatrix}

then

\displaystyle{\chi^{\dagger}}{T^{c}}{T^{b}}{X_{\rm{M}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{{\sqrt{2}}}\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{{\chi_{2}}-i{\chi_{1}}}&{{\chi_{0}}+i{\chi_{3}}}\end{array}}\right)\frac{\nu}{{2\sqrt{2}}}\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{{\delta_{c3}}{\delta_{b1}}-{\delta_{c1}}{\delta_{b3}}+i\left({{\delta_{c2}}{\delta_{b3}}-{\delta_{c3}}{\delta_{b2}}}\right)}\\
{{\delta_{ci}}{\delta_{bi}}+i\left({{\delta_{c2}}{\delta_{b1}}-{\delta_{c1}}{\delta_{b2}}}\right)}\end{array}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\nu}{4}\Big\{{\chi_{2}}\left({{\delta_{c3}}{\delta_{b1}}-{\delta_{c1}}{\delta_{b3}}}\right)+{\chi_{1}}\left({{\delta_{c2}}{\delta_{b3}}-{\delta_{c3}}{\delta_{b2}}}\right)+i\left[{{\chi_{2}}\left({{\delta_{c2}}{\delta_{b3}}-{\delta_{c3}}{\delta_{b2}}}\right)-{\chi_{1}}\left({{\delta_{c3}}{\delta_{b1}}-{\delta_{c1}}{\delta_{b3}}}\right)}\right]+

\displaystyle+{\chi_{0}}\left({{\delta_{ci}}{\delta_{bi}}}\right)-{\chi_{3}}\left({{\delta_{c2}}{\delta_{b1}}-{\delta_{c1}}{\delta_{b2}}}\right)+i\left[{{\chi_{0}}\left({{\delta_{c2}}{\delta_{b1}}-{\delta_{c1}}{\delta_{b2}}}\right)+{\chi_{3}}\left({{\delta_{ci}}{\delta_{bi}}}\right)}\right]\Big\}

(4.1.33)

Taking the hermitian conjugate of the above expression and substituting into the last two terms of (
4.1.27
) we obtain

\displaystyle{\chi^{\dagger}}{T^{c}}{T^{b}}{X_{\rm{M}}}+X_{\rm{M}}^{\dagger}{T^{b}}{T^{c}}\chi=\frac{1}{2}\nu\left\{{{\chi_{2}}\left({{\delta_{c3}}{\delta_{b1}}-{\delta_{c1}}{\delta_{b3}}}\right)+{\chi_{1}}\left({{\delta_{c2}}{\delta_{b3}}-{\delta_{c3}}{\delta_{b2}}}\right)-{\chi_{3}}\left({{\delta_{c2}}{\delta_{b1}}-{\delta_{c1}}{\delta_{b2}}}\right)+{\chi_{0}}\left({{\delta_{ci}}{\delta_{bi}}}\right)}\right\}

(4.1.34)

A closer observation of the above expression and we can see the anti-symmetric pattern present in the first three terns. This can be made clearer by using the Levi-Civita symbol

\displaystyle{\chi^{\dagger}}{T^{c}}{T^{b}}{X_{\rm{M}}}+X_{\rm{M}}^{\dagger}{T^{b}}{T^{c}}\chi

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\nu\left({{\chi_{a}}{\varepsilon_{alm}}{\delta_{cl}}{\delta_{bm}}+{\chi_{0}}{\delta_{bc}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\nu\left({{\chi_{0}}{\delta_{bc}}-{\chi_{a}}{\varepsilon_{abc}}}\right)

(4.1.35)

Secondly, a more elegant way to see this result is to make use of the commutation and anti-commutation relation between the sigma matrices,

\displaystyle\left[{{\sigma^{b}},{\sigma^{c}}}\right]=2i{\varepsilon_{abc}}{\sigma^{a}},\quad\left\{{{\sigma^{b}},{\sigma^{c}}}\right\}=2{\delta_{bc}}

(4.1.36)

which when summed give an expression for the product of the sigma matrices in terms of the Levi-Civita symbol

\displaystyle{\sigma^{b}}{\sigma^{c}}

\displaystyle={\delta_{bc}}+i{\varepsilon_{abc}}{\sigma^{a}}

\displaystyle\implies{T^{b}}{T^{c}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}{\delta_{bc}}+\frac{1}{4}i{\varepsilon_{abc}}{\sigma^{a}}

(4.1.37)

So

\displaystyle X_{\rm{M}}^{\dagger}{T^{b}}{T^{c}}\chi=\frac{1}{4}{\delta^{bc}}X_{\rm{M}}^{\dagger}\chi+\frac{1}{4}i{\varepsilon_{abc}}X_{\rm{M}}^{\dagger}{\sigma^{a}}\chi

(4.1.38)

and the pieces (can be obtain using parameterized sigma matrices), are given by

\displaystyle X_{\rm{M}}^{\dagger}\chi

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{{\sqrt{2}}}\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}0&{2\nu}\end{array}}\right)\frac{1}{{\sqrt{2}}}\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{{\chi_{2}}+i{\chi_{1}}}\\
{{\chi_{0}}-i{\chi_{3}}}\end{array}}\right)

(4.1.39)

\displaystyle=\left({{\chi_{0}}-i{\chi_{3}}}\right)\nu

\displaystyle X_{\rm{M}}^{\dagger}{\sigma^{a}}\chi

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{{\sqrt{2}}}\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}0&{2\nu}\end{array}}\right)\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{{\delta_{a3}}}&{{\delta_{a1}}-i{\delta_{a2}}}\\
{{\delta_{a1}}+i{\delta_{a2}}}&{-{\delta_{a3}}}\end{array}}\right)\frac{1}{{\sqrt{2}}}\left({\begin{array}[]{*{20}{c}}{{\chi_{2}}+i{\chi_{1}}}\\
{{\chi_{0}}-i{\chi_{3}}}\end{array}}\right)

\displaystyle=\nu\left[{i\left({{\chi_{m}}{\delta_{am}}}\right)+{\chi_{2}}{\delta_{a1}}-{\chi_{1}}{\delta_{a2}}-{\chi_{0}}{\delta_{a3}}}\right]

(4.1.40)

The result for the
X_{\rm{M}}^{\dagger}{T^{b}}{T^{c}}\chi
product is given by

\displaystyle X_{\rm{M}}^{\dagger}{T^{b}}{T^{c}}\chi=\frac{1}{4}{\delta^{bc}}\left({{\chi_{0}}-i{\chi_{3}}}\right)\nu+\frac{1}{4}\nu\left[{-{\chi_{a}}{\varepsilon_{abc}}+i\left({{\chi_{2}}{\varepsilon_{1bc}}-{\chi_{1}}{\varepsilon_{2bc}}-{\chi_{0}}{\varepsilon_{3bc}}}\right)}\right]

Summing the above with its hermitian conjugate yields

\displaystyle{\chi^{\dagger}}{T^{c}}{T^{b}}{X_{\rm{M}}}+X_{\rm{M}}^{\dagger}{T^{b}}{T^{c}}\chi=\frac{1}{2}\nu\left({{\chi_{0}}{\delta^{bc}}-{\chi_{a}}{\varepsilon_{abc}}}\right)

(4.1.41)

which is the same result from the brute force computations. Substituting (
4.1.29
) and (
4.1.41
) into (
4.1.27
)

\displaystyle\delta\left({X_{\rm{M}}^{\dagger}{T^{b}}\chi-{\chi^{\dagger}}{T^{b}}{X_{\rm{M}}}}\right)

\displaystyle=i\delta{\alpha^{c}}\left[{X_{\rm{M}}^{\dagger}\left\{{{T^{b}},{T^{c}}}\right\}{X_{\rm{M}}}+X_{\rm{M}}^{\dagger}{T^{b}}{T^{c}}\chi+{\chi^{\dagger}}{T^{c}}{T^{b}}{X_{\rm{M}}}}\right]

\displaystyle=i\delta{\alpha^{c}}\left[{{\nu^{2}}{\delta^{bc}}+\frac{1}{2}\nu\left({{\chi_{0}}{\delta^{bc}}-{\chi_{a}}{\varepsilon_{abc}}}\right)}\right]

(4.1.42)

We have gathered all the ingredients to compute the matrix
M^{bc}
, substituting (
4.1.23
) and (
4.1.42
) into (
4.1.15
)

\displaystyle{M^{bc}}

\displaystyle=\frac{{\delta{G^{b}}\left[{{}^{\alpha}A;x}\right]}}{{\delta{\alpha^{c}}\left(y\right)}}

\displaystyle=\int{{d^{4}}z\left[{\partial_{x}^{\mu}{\delta_{bd}}\delta\left({x-z}\right)g_{\mu}^{\nu}\frac{{\delta A_{\nu}^{d}\left(z\right)}}{{\delta{\alpha^{c}}\left(y\right)}}}\right]}+\xi ie\frac{\delta}{{\delta{\alpha^{c}}\left(y\right)}}\left({X_{\rm{M}}^{\dagger}{T^{b}}\chi-{\chi^{\dagger}}{T^{b}}{X_{\rm{M}}}}\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{e}\partial_{x}^{\mu}\int{{d^{4}}z\left[{\delta\left({x-z}\right)D_{\mu}^{bc}\delta\left({z-y}\right)}\right]}-\xi e\left[{{\nu^{2}}{\delta^{bc}}+\frac{1}{2}\nu\left({{\chi_{0}}{\delta^{bc}}-{\chi_{a}}{\varepsilon_{abc}}}\right)}\right]\frac{{\delta{\alpha^{c}}\left(x\right)}}{{\delta{\alpha^{c}}\left(y\right)}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{e}\partial_{x}^{\mu}D_{\mu}^{bc}\delta\left({x-y}\right)-\xi e\left[{{\nu^{2}}{\delta^{bc}}+\frac{1}{2}\nu\left({{\chi_{0}}{\delta^{bc}}-{\chi_{a}}{\varepsilon_{abc}}}\right)}\right]\delta\left({x-y}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{e}\left[{-\partial_{x}^{\mu}D_{\mu}^{bc}-\xi{e^{2}}{\nu^{2}}{\delta^{bc}}-\frac{1}{2}\xi{e^{2}}\nu{\chi_{0}}{\delta^{bc}}+\frac{1}{2}\xi{e^{2}}\nu{\chi_{a}}{\varepsilon_{abc}}}\right]\delta\left({x-y}\right)

(4.1.43)

Note that in the third line we have moved the
\partial_{x}^{\mu}
out of the integral since it acts only on the
\delta(x-z)
. The derivative of the covariant derivative in the adjoint representation is

\displaystyle\partial_{x}^{\mu}D_{\mu}^{bc}=\partial_{x}^{\mu}\left[{{\delta^{bc}}{\partial_{x\mu}}+e{\varepsilon_{bac}}A_{\mu}^{a}}\right]

(4.1.44)

where the
\partial_{x}^{\mu}
is understood to act on everything to its right. We are still left with the problem of constructing determinant of matrix
M^{bc}
. In its current form the usefulness of the path integral,

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}=\int{\mathcal{D}A{e^{iS\left[A\right]}}{e^{-\frac{i}{{2\xi}}\int{{d^{4}}x}G_{a}^{2}\left(A\right)}}\Delta\left[A\right]}

(4.1.45)

is limited since functional techniques are limited to functionals of Gaussian form and the determinant spoils this form. This can be
remedied by noting that Berezin-type functional integrals
[
41
]
over Grassmann variables results in a determinant in the numerator i.e.

\displaystyle\Delta\left[A\right]

\displaystyle=\det\left[{{M^{bc}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\int{\mathcal{D}\bar{u}\mathcal{D}u{e^{-ie\int{{d^{4}}x{d^{4}}y{{\bar{u}}^{b}}\left(x\right){M^{bc}}\left({x,y}\right){u^{c}}\left(y\right)}}}}

(4.1.46)

Where
\bar{u}^{a},{u}^{a}
are anti-commuting Grassmann fields with

\displaystyle\left\{{{u^{a}},{u^{b}}}\right\}=\left\{{{{\bar{u}}^{a}},{u^{b}}}\right\}=\left\{{{{\bar{u}}^{a}},{{\bar{u}}^{b}}}\right\}=0

(4.1.47)

Since the expressions are long we shall write down the pieces separately

\displaystyle-ie\int{d^{4}}x{d^{4}}y{{\bar{u}}^{b}}\left(x\right){M^{bc}}\left({x,y}\right){u^{c}}\left(y\right)

\displaystyle=-ie\int{{d^{4}}x{d^{4}}y}{{\bar{u}}^{b}}\left(x\right)\frac{1}{e}\Biggl[-\partial_{x}^{\mu}D_{\mu}^{bc}-\xi{e^{2}}{\nu^{2}}{\delta^{bc}}-\frac{1}{2}\xi{e^{2}}\nu{\chi_{0}}{\delta^{bc}}+

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\xi{e^{2}}\nu{\chi_{a}}{\varepsilon_{abc}}\Biggr]\delta\left({x-y}\right){u^{c}}\left(y\right)

\displaystyle=i\int{{d^{4}}x}{{\bar{u}}^{b}}\left(x\right)\left[{\partial_{x}^{\mu}D_{\mu}^{bc}+\xi{e^{2}}{\nu^{2}}{\delta^{bc}}+\frac{1}{2}\xi{e^{2}}\nu{\chi_{0}}{\delta^{bc}}-\frac{1}{2}\xi{e^{2}}\nu{\chi_{a}}{\varepsilon_{abc}}}\right]{u^{c}}\left(x\right)

(4.1.48)

Substituting in the covariant derivative in the adjoint representation for

\displaystyle{{\bar{u}}^{b}}\left[{\partial_{x}^{\mu}D_{\mu}^{bc}+\xi{e^{2}}{\nu^{2}}{\delta^{bc}}}\right]{u^{c}}

\displaystyle={{\bar{u}}^{b}}\left[{{\partial^{\mu}}\left({{\delta^{bc}}{\partial_{\mu}}+e{\varepsilon_{bac}}A_{\mu}^{a}}\right)+\xi{e^{2}}{\nu^{2}}{\delta^{bc}}}\right]{u^{c}}

\displaystyle={{\bar{u}}^{a}}\left({{\partial^{\mu}}{\partial_{\mu}}+\xi{e^{2}}{\nu^{2}}}\right){u^{a}}+e{\varepsilon_{bac}}{{\bar{u}}^{b}}{\partial^{\mu}}\left({A_{\mu}^{a}{u^{c}}}\right)

\displaystyle={{\bar{u}}^{a}}\left({{\partial^{\mu}}{\partial_{\mu}}+\xi{e^{2}}{\nu^{2}}}\right){u^{a}}+e{\varepsilon_{abc}}A_{\mu}^{a}\left({{\partial^{\mu}}{{\bar{u}}^{b}}}\right){u^{c}}

(4.1.49)

Putting all the pieces together for the exponent

\displaystyle-ie\int{{d^{4}}x{d^{4}}y{{\bar{u}}^{b}}\left(x\right){M^{bc}}\left({x,y}\right){u^{c}}\left(y\right)}

\displaystyle=i\int{{d^{4}}x}\Biggl[{{\bar{u}}^{a}}\left({{\partial^{\mu}}{\partial_{\mu}}+\xi{e^{2}}{\nu^{2}}}\right){u^{a}}+e{\varepsilon_{abc}}A_{\mu}^{a}\left({{\partial^{\mu}}{{\bar{u}}^{b}}}\right){u^{c}}+\frac{1}{2}\xi{e^{2}}\nu{\chi_{0}}{{\bar{u}}^{a}}{u^{a}}+

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\xi{e^{2}}\nu{\varepsilon_{abc}}{\chi_{a}}{{\bar{u}}^{b}}{u^{c}}\Biggr]

(4.1.50)

The determinant can now be expressed as

\displaystyle\Delta\left[A\right]

\displaystyle=\int{\mathcal{D}\bar{u}\mathcal{D}u{e^{-ie\int{{d^{4}}x{d^{4}}y{{\bar{u}}^{b}}\left(x\right){M^{bc}}\left({x,y}\right){u^{c}}\left(y\right)}}}}

\displaystyle=\int{\mathcal{D}\bar{u}\mathcal{D}u{e^{i\int{{d^{4}}x}\left[{{{\bar{u}}^{a}}\left({{\partial^{\mu}}{\partial_{\mu}}+\xi{e^{2}}{\nu^{2}}}\right){u^{a}}+e{\varepsilon_{abc}}A_{\mu}^{a}\left({{\partial^{\mu}}{{\bar{u}}^{b}}}\right){u^{c}}+\frac{1}{2}\xi{e^{2}}\nu{\chi_{0}}{{\bar{u}}^{a}}{u^{a}}-\frac{1}{2}\xi{e^{2}}\nu{\varepsilon_{abc}}{\chi_{a}}{{\bar{u}}^{b}}{u^{c}}}\right]}}}

(4.1.51)

Substituting the determinant into the path integral

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}

\displaystyle=\int{\mathcal{D}A{e^{iS\left[A\right]}}{e^{-\frac{i}{{2\xi}}\int{{d^{4}}x}G_{a}^{2}\left(A\right)}}\Delta\left[A\right]}

\displaystyle=\int{\mathcal{D}\bar{u}\mathcal{D}u\mathcal{D}A}{e^{iS\left[A\right]-\frac{i}{{2\xi}}\int{{d^{4}}x}G_{a}^{2}\left(A\right)+i\int{{d^{4}}x}\left[{{{\bar{u}}^{a}}\left({{\partial^{\mu}}{\partial_{\mu}}+\xi{e^{2}}{\nu^{2}}}\right){u^{a}}+e{\varepsilon_{abc}}A_{\mu}^{a}\left({{\partial^{\mu}}{{\bar{u}}^{b}}}\right){u^{c}}+\frac{1}{2}\xi{e^{2}}\nu{\chi_{0}}{{\bar{u}}^{a}}{u^{a}}-\frac{1}{2}\xi{e^{2}}\nu{\varepsilon_{abc}}{\chi_{a}}{{\bar{u}}^{b}}{u^{c}}}\right]}}

\displaystyle=\int{\mathcal{D}\bar{u}\mathcal{D}u\mathcal{D}A}{e^{i\int{{d^{4}}x}\left[{\mathscr{L}-\frac{1}{{2\xi}}G_{a}^{2}\left(A\right)+{{\bar{u}}^{a}}\left({{\partial^{\mu}}{\partial_{\mu}}+\xi{e^{2}}{\nu^{2}}}\right){u^{a}}+e{\varepsilon_{abc}}A_{\mu}^{a}\left({{\partial^{\mu}}{{\bar{u}}^{b}}}\right){u^{c}}+\frac{1}{2}\xi{e^{2}}\nu{\chi_{0}}{{\bar{u}}^{a}}{u^{a}}-\frac{1}{2}\xi{e^{2}}\nu{\varepsilon_{abc}}{\chi_{a}}{{\bar{u}}^{b}}{u^{c}}}\right]}}

\displaystyle=\int{\mathcal{D}\bar{u}\mathcal{D}u\mathcal{D}A}{e^{i\int{{d^{4}}x{\mathscr{L}_{{\rm{eff}}}}}}}

(4.1.52)

Where we have defined the effective Lagrangian

\displaystyle{\mathscr{L}_{{\rm{eff}}}}:=\mathscr{L}-\frac{1}{{2\xi}}G_{a}^{2}\left(A\right)+{{\bar{u}}^{a}}\left({{\partial^{\mu}}{\partial_{\mu}}+\xi{e^{2}}{\nu^{2}}}\right){u^{a}}+e{\varepsilon_{abc}}A_{\mu}^{a}\left({{\partial^{\mu}}{{\bar{u}}^{b}}}\right){u^{c}}+\frac{1}{2}\xi{e^{2}}\nu{\chi_{0}}{{\bar{u}}^{a}}{u^{a}}-\frac{1}{2}\xi{e^{2}}\nu{\varepsilon_{abc}}{\chi_{a}}{{\bar{u}}^{b}}{u^{c}}

(4.1.53)

4.2
Gauge Fixing Term

We give a justification of the choice of the gauge fixing function in (
4.1.14
) shown here:

\displaystyle{G^{b}}\left[{{}^{\alpha}A;x}\right]={\partial^{\mu}}A_{\mu}^{b}+\xi ie\left({X_{\rm{M}}^{\dagger}{T^{b}}\chi-{\chi^{\dagger}}{T^{b}}X_{\rm{M}}}\right)

After spontaneous symmetry breaking, some pesky two point mixing terms (
3.1.30
)

\displaystyle X_{\rm{M}}^{\dagger}{\partial^{\mu}}{A_{\mu}}\chi-{\chi^{\dagger}}{\partial^{\mu}}{A_{\mu}}{X_{\rm{M}}}=\frac{i}{{\sqrt{2}}}{\chi_{a}}{\partial^{\mu}}A_{\mu}^{a}

were generated which were hard to interpret. These terms survived and were present in the Higgs lagrangian shown below:

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{H}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left({{\partial_{\mu}}H{\partial^{\mu}}H-{\mu^{2}}{H^{2}}}\right)+\frac{1}{2}{\partial_{\mu}}{\chi_{a}}{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi_{a}}+ie{\partial^{\mu}}A_{\mu}^{a}\left[{X_{\rm{M}}^{\dagger}{T^{a}}\chi-{\chi^{\dagger}}{T^{a}}{X_{\rm{M}}}}\right]-\frac{1}{4}e{\varepsilon_{abc}}\left({{\chi_{a}}\overleftrightarrow{\partial^{\mu}}{\chi_{b}}}\right)A_{\mu}^{c}+

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}e\left({{\chi_{a}}\overleftrightarrow{\partial^{\mu}}H}\right)A_{\mu}^{a}+\frac{1}{2}{e^{2}}{\nu^{2}}A_{\mu}^{a}{A^{a\mu}}+\frac{1}{2}{e^{2}}\nu HA_{\mu}^{a}{A^{a\mu}}+\frac{1}{8}{e^{2}}{H^{2}}A_{\mu}^{a}{A^{a\mu}}+\frac{1}{8}{e^{2}}\chi_{b}^{2}A_{\mu}^{a}{A^{a\mu}}-\frac{\lambda}{{32}}{H^{4}}-\frac{\lambda}{{16}}{H^{2}}\chi_{a}^{2}+
